约翰·埃格顿没再说什么，跟了出去。那个没说出口却照亮了一切的词——「谋杀」——既让他看清了这桩事情的全部可怕，也让他看清了眼下十万火急：必须帮斯蒂芬「把她弄走」。为了玛杰丽·伯恩。至于他此刻对斯蒂芬作何感想，倘若斯蒂芬是个单身汉他又会怎么做——他来不及细想。也无关紧要。为了伯恩太太——为了玛杰丽——总得想个办法，正如斯蒂芬所说。而他，约翰·埃格顿，必须搭把手。

===Original===

“What are you going to do?” he said.

Stephen was crouched on his haunches, busily tidying Emily’s nightdress, pulling it about.

“The river,” he said shortly. “It’s high tide﻿—Thank God!” he added.

===Chinese===

「你打算怎么办？」他问。

斯蒂芬蹲着，正忙着替埃米莉掖弄睡衣，拉来扯去。

「扔河里。」他简短地说。「正是满潮——感谢上帝！」他补了一句。

===Original===

John Egerton looked shrinkingly at the torn and ineffective nightdress, at the wide spaces of pink flesh showing through the rents. He could not imagine himself picking up that body. He said, “What?﻿—like﻿—like *that*?”

Stephen looked up. “Yes,” he said; “why not?” But he knew very well why not. Because of a certain insane sense of decency which governs even a murderer in the presence of death. Emily Gaunt must not be “got away” like that! Besides, it would be dangerous. He thought for a moment. Then, “No,” he said. “Wait a minute,” and clattered down the basement stairs.

When he came back he was trailing behind him a long and capacious sack, which had hung on a nail in the scullery for the receipt of waste paper and bottles and odds and ends of domestic refuse. The sack, fortunately, had been only half full. All its contents he had tumbled recklessly on the scullery floor. But as he came up the stairs he was curiously disturbed by the thought of that refuse. What was to be done with it? What would Margery say? The scullery had been recently cleaned out, he knew. And the sack? How could he explain its disappearance? These damned details.

===Chinese===

约翰·埃格顿畏畏缩缩地望着那件撕破了的、顶不了事的睡衣，望着破洞里露出来的大片大片粉色肌肤。他没法想象自己动手去抱那具尸体。他说：「什么？——就——就这样*光着*？」

斯蒂芬抬起头。「是啊，」他说；「有什么不行？」可他心里清楚得很，为什么不行。因为面对死者时，连杀人凶手也受着某种疯狂的体面感的主宰。总不能让埃米莉·冈特就那样「弄走」！再说，那样也危险。他想了片刻。然后说：「不行。等等。」随即蹬蹬蹬跑下地下室的楼梯。

他回来时，身后拖着一个长长的大口袋。那口袋原挂在洗碗间一枚钉子上，专门收废纸、瓶子和各种家居垃圾。所幸口袋只半满。他把里头的东西不管不顾，一股脑儿全倒在洗碗间地上。可上楼的时候，那堆垃圾却莫名地搅得他心神不宁。怎么处置？玛杰丽会怎么说？他知道，洗碗间前不久才清扫过。那口袋呢？它没了，怎么交代？这些该死的细节。

===Original===

“Here you are,” he said. “This will do,” and he laid the sack on the floor.

He began to put Emily into the sack. He drew the mouth of the sack over her feet. They were already cold. John Egerton stood stiffly under the light, in a kind of paralysis of disgust. He felt “I must help!﻿ ﻿… I must help!” but somehow he could not move a finger.

The sack was over the knees now. It was strangely difficult. The toes kept catching.

===Chinese===

「喏。」他说。「这个管用。」说着把口袋铺在地板上。

他动手把埃米莉装进口袋。他把袋口往她脚上套。双脚已经凉了。约翰·埃格顿僵直地站在灯下，厌恶得近乎瘫软。他心里喊着「我得帮忙！……我得帮忙！」可不知怎的，一根指头也动不了。

口袋已套过膝盖。这活儿出奇地难。脚趾老往外钩。

===Original===

But Stephen was fantastically preoccupied with the refuse on the scullery floor, with coming explanations about the sack. “There’ll be an awful row,” he said﻿ ﻿… “the hell of a mess down there﻿ ﻿… what shall I say about the sack?” Then, suddenly, “What shall I say, John?﻿ ﻿… Think of something, for God’s sake!”

John Egerton jumped. The wild incongruity of Stephen’s question scarcely occurred to him. He tried solemnly to think of something to say about the sack. He would be helpful here, surely. But no thought came. His mind was a confused muddle of nightdresses and inquests and naked legs and Margery Byrne﻿—Margery Byrne arriving quietly on the doorstep﻿—Margery Byrne scandalized, agonized, hideously, fatally ill.

“I don’t know, Stephen,” he said feebly﻿—“I don’t know﻿ ﻿… say you﻿ ﻿… oh, anything.”

===Chinese===

可斯蒂芬的心思却荒唐地拴在洗碗间地上那堆垃圾上，拴在日后怎么交代那只口袋上。「准有一场大闹，」他说……「楼下弄得一塌糊涂……口袋的事我怎么交代？」接着，突然：「我怎么说，约翰？……看在上帝份上，快想个说法！」

约翰·埃格顿一激灵。斯蒂芬这问题问得有多离谱，他几乎浑然不觉。他郑重其事地替口袋想说法。这回他总算能帮上忙了吧。可脑子里空空如也。他的头脑成了一锅粥：睡衣、验尸审讯、光溜溜的腿、玛杰丽·伯恩——玛杰丽·伯恩悄没声儿地站上门阶——玛杰丽·伯恩惊骇、绞痛、病得凶险、病得要命。

「我不知道，斯蒂芬。」他有气无力地说——「我不知道……就说你……唉，怎么说都行。」

===Original===

He was fascinated now by the progress of the sack, which had nearly covered the legs. He saw clearly that a moment was coming when he would *have* to help, when one of them would have to lift Emily and one of them manipulate the sack. Already Stephen was cursing and in difficulties. The nightdress kept rucking up and had to be pulled back, and when that was done the sack lost ground again.

“Oh, hell!” he said, with a note of final exasperation, “lend a hand, John﻿—lift her a bit,” and then as John still hesitated, sick with reluctance, “Oh, *lift* her, can’t you?”

John stooped down. The moment had come. He put his hands under the small of Emily’s back, shuddering as he touched her. With an effort he lifted her an inch or two. With a great heave Stephen advanced the sack six inches. Then it caught again in those maddening toes. With a guttural exclamation of rage he turned back towards the feet and tugged furiously at the sack. When it was free John Egerton had relaxed his hold. Emily was lying heavy on the slack of the sack. He was gazing with a kind of helpless horror at the purple inflammation of Emily’s throat, realizing for the first time just how brutal and violent her end had been.

===Chinese===

这会儿，口袋的进展倒把他攫住了：两条腿差不多罩全了。他清清楚楚地看见，那个时刻正在逼近，他*非*帮忙不可的时刻——总得一个把埃米莉抬起来，另一个去摆弄口袋。斯蒂芬已经在骂骂咧咧、手忙脚乱了。睡衣不停地往上缩，得拉下来；拉好了，口袋又败退下去。

「哦，见鬼！」他说，嗓音里带着忍到头的火气，「搭把手，约翰——把她抬一下。」见约翰还在犹豫，腻烦得直犯恶心，他又说：「哦，*抬*她呀，行不行？」

约翰弯下腰去。时刻到了。他把双手垫到埃米莉的腰眼下，一碰着她便打了个寒颤。他使劲把她抬起一两寸。斯蒂芬大喝一声用力，把口袋推进六寸。接着袋口又让那对气人的脚趾钩住了。他喉咙里咕噜一声怒骂，转身扑向那双脚，发狠地拽口袋。等拽脱了，约翰·埃格顿的手早松开了。埃米莉沉沉压在口袋的松褶上。他正满眼无助的惊恐，盯着埃米莉喉间紫红的伤痕，头一回真切地体会到，她的死法有多么粗暴，多么暴烈。

===Original===

Stephen cursed again. “Lift, damn you, *lift*﻿—oh, hell!”

John lifted, and with a wild fumbling impatience the whole of Emily’s body was covered. Only the head and one arm were left. They had forgotten the arm. It lay flung out away from the body, half hidden under an overcoat. Stephen seized it savagely and tried to bend it in under the mouth of the sack, with brutal ridiculous tugs, like an ill-tempered man packing an overloaded bag. John watched him with growing disapproval.

“That’s no good,” he said. “Pull down the sack again.”

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬又骂开了。「抬呀，该死的，*抬*——哦，见鬼！」

约翰抬起，两人慌手慌脚、急不可耐，总算把埃米莉全身都罩进了口袋。只剩脑袋和一条胳膊。他们把那条胳膊忘了。它直挺挺甩在尸身外头，半埋在一件外套底下。斯蒂芬狠狠攥住它，想把它折进袋口里去，又粗暴又可笑地拽个不停，像个坏脾气的汉子硬塞一只塞爆了的提包。约翰看着，越看越皱眉。

「那样不行的。」他说。「把口袋再拉下来。」

===Original===

Stephen did so. The sweat now was running down his face; he was spent and panting, and his composure was all gone. With his black hair ruffled over his forehead he looked wicked.

Something of his impatience had communicated itself to John, mastering even his abhorrence. He wanted furiously to get the thing done. It was he now who seized the recalcitrant arm and thrust it into the sack; it was he who fiercely pulled the sack over Emily’s head, and hid at last that puffy and appalling face with a long “Ah‑h” of relief. At the mouth of the sack was a fortunate piece of cord, threaded through a circle of ragged holes.

John Egerton pulled it tight and fumbled at the making of a knot. He felt vaguely that something special in the way of knots was required﻿—a bowline﻿—a reef knot or something﻿—not a “granny,” anyhow. How was it you tied a reef knot? Dimly remembered instructions came to him﻿—“the same string over both times”﻿—or “under,” wasn’t it?

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬照办了。这会儿汗水正顺着他的脸往下淌；他筋疲力尽，气喘吁吁，从容荡然无存。乌黑的头发乱蓬蓬支棱在额前，他一脸凶相。

他那股急躁劲儿多少传给了约翰，压倒了他的憎恶。他也狂热地只想赶紧了结。这会儿，正是他一把抓住那条不听话的胳膊塞进口袋；正是他狠狠把口袋拉过埃米莉的头，总算把那张浮肿骇人的脸遮住，长长地「啊——」了一声，如释重负。袋口恰好有一截绳头，穿在一圈磨破的孔眼里。

约翰·埃格顿把绳抽紧，笨手笨脚打起结束。他恍惚觉得，这结非同小可，得打个像样的结——单套结——平结还是什么——反正不能是「祖母结」。平结是怎么打来着？记忆深处浮起几句口诀——「两头都从上面绕」——还是「下面」，是不是？

===Original===

Stephen crouched at his side, dazedly watching his mobile fingers muddling with the cord.

A step sounded outside on the pavement. Stephen woke up with a whispered “My God!” and panic snatched at the pair of them. Feverishly John finished his knot and tugged at the ends. It was a “granny,” he saw, but a granny it must remain. The steps had surely stopped outside the door.

“Quick,” he whispered, and got his right arm under the sack. Stumbling and straining, with a reckless disturbance of rugs and mats, they bundled the sagging body of Emily Gaunt into the dining-room. In the dining-room John Egerton halted and laid his end of her down. He was not strong, and she was heavy. Stephen clung to her feet, and the two of them stood listening, very shaky and afraid. There was no sound in the street now. The steps must have passed the door. From the rear there was the melancholy hooting of a tug, calling for its waiting barges at Ginger Wharf. They could hear the slow, methodical panting of her engines and the furtive swish of the water at her bows. In the garden a cat was wailing﻿—horribly like a child in pain. To John Egerton these familiar sounds seemed like the noises of a new world, the new world he had entered at about a quarter-past nine, when he had become a partner, an accomplice, in this wretched piece of brutality and deceit. He felt curiously identified with it now﻿—he was part of it, not merely an impersonal observer. He had a sensation of personal guilt.

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬蹲在他身旁，恍恍惚惚地看着他那灵巧的手指跟绳子纠缠。

人行道上响起一阵脚步声。斯蒂芬低呼一声「我的上帝！」，猛醒过来，恐慌把两人一齐攥住。约翰发了烧似的打完结，拽紧绳头。他看出来了，是个「祖母结」——可祖母结就只好祖母结了。脚步声肯定在门外停住了。

「快。」他悄声说，右臂伸到口袋底下。两人跌跌撞撞、连拖带拽，全然不顾踩乱的小地毯和地席，把埃米莉·冈特软塌塌的身子塞进了餐厅。到了餐厅，约翰·埃格顿停下脚步，放下他那一头。他不算有劲，而她很沉。斯蒂芬抱着她的双脚，两人站在那里侧耳听，腿肚子直打晃。街上这时没有一点声音。脚步想必已经走过了门前。屋后传来拖船凄凉的汽笛——它在金杰码头（Ginger Wharf）召唤等候的驳船。他们听得见那机器缓慢而按部就班的喘息，听得见船头鬼祟的水声。花园里有只猫在哀嚎——像孩子在受痛，像得吓人。在约翰·埃格顿听来，这些熟悉的声音竟像是另一个世界的动静——那个他九点一刻前后踏进的新世界，从那一刻起，他成了这桩可悲的暴行与欺骗的合伙人、同谋。此刻他奇异地觉得自己与它融为一体——他是它的一部分，不再是无关痛痒的旁观者。他有一种切身的罪感。

===Original===

“It’s all right,” said somebody, very far away, in the voice of Stephen Byrne﻿—a hoarse and furtive voice.

John Egerton picked up his burden, and another staggering stage was accomplished into the conservatory.

It was dusk now, but a large moon was up, and thin streams of silver filtered through the opaque roof and the crowded vine-leaves on to the long bundle on the floor. It was too light, Stephen thought, for this kind of work.

===Chinese===

「没事了。」有人说话了，声音极远，是斯蒂芬·伯恩的嗓音——嘶哑，鬼祟。

约翰·埃格顿重新扛起他那份重负，又踉踉跄跄挪完一程，进了花房。

这时天已擦黑，可一轮大月亮升了起来，细细的银流透过不透光的玻璃顶棚和密密层层的葡萄叶，筛落在地板上那个长长的包袱上。干这种勾当，这光线未免太亮了，斯蒂芬想。

===Original===

When they had halted he said, “Wait a minute, John﻿—I’ll go and see if the coast is clear.” He went quickly down the stone steps into the tiny garden. The long, rich grass of Stephen’s “lawn” was drenched and glistening with dew. There was the heavy scent of something in the next-door garden, and over all a hot, intolerable stillness. Stephen became suddenly oppressed with the sense of guilt. Instinctively he stepped on to the wet grass and rustled softly through it to the river, his silk socks sponging up the dew.

Over the shallow wall he inspected furtively the silent river. Nothing moved. It was slack water, and the downward procession of tugs had not properly begun. The water was smooth; the black reflections of the opposite trees were sharp and perfect. Down towards Hammersmith a few lights hung like pendant jewels in the water. Over the far houses there was a flicker like summer lightning from an electric train. A huddle of driftwood and odd refuse floated motionless in midstream, very black and visible, waiting for the tide to turn; but along the edges the stream already crept stealthily down, lapping softly against the moored ranks of boats, against Stephen’s boat riding comfortably beneath him. In the neighbouring gardens nothing moved. About this hour in the hot weather the residents of Hammerton Chase would creep out secretly into their gardens and cast their refuse into the river, and there was often to be heard at dusk a scattered succession of subdued splashes.

But tonight there were no splashes. Probably the duty was already done. Stephen remembered incongruously this local habit, and was at once relieved and disappointed. Too many people prowling in their gardens might be dangerous. On the other hand, there was a certain safety in a multitude of splashes. One more would have made no difference.

===Chinese===

两人停下后，他说：「等一下，约翰——我去看看外面动静。」他快步走下石阶，进了小花园。斯蒂芬那片「草坪」上茂密的长草浸透了露水，亮晶晶的。隔壁花园里有什么东西香气浓重，四下里罩着一层闷热难当的死寂。斯蒂芬心头猛地被罪感压住。他本能地踏上湿草，窸窸窣窣穿行到河边，丝袜吸饱了露水。

他隔着矮墙，偷偷察看寂静的河面。什么都没动。正是平潮，成串下行的拖船还没有正式开拔。水面平滑；对岸树木的黑色倒影清晰而完整。往哈默史密斯方向，几盏灯火像垂坠的珠宝悬在水里。远处的房屋上空闪过一道光，像夏日闪电——是电气列车驶过。一堆浮木和乱七八糟的垃圾一动不动漂在河心，黑黢黢的，格外显眼，等着潮水转向；沿岸的水却已经悄悄爬着下行，轻轻拍打着系泊成排的船只，拍打着斯蒂芬脚下安安稳稳浮着的自家小船。左邻右舍的花园里毫无动静。天热的时节，每到这个钟点，哈默顿蔡斯的住户们总会悄悄溜进自家花园，把垃圾抛进河里，黄昏时常能听见一阵零落而压抑的扑通声。

可今夜没有扑通声。这桩例行公事想必已经办完。斯蒂芬不合时宜地想起这个本地习惯，一时如释重负，一时又觉失望。太多人在花园里逡巡，也许危险。反过来，扑通声此起彼伏倒自有一种安全。多一声，不多不少，谁也听不出来。

===Original===

There were no splashes now, and scarcely any sound: only the fretful muttering of distant traffic, the occasional rumble of buses on the far-off bridge, and the small plops of fishes leaping at the moon. Close to Stephen was an unobtrusive munching in the wired space where Joan’s rabbits were kept. A buck rabbit lay hunched in the moonlight masticating contentedly the last remnants of the evening cabbage. Another nosed at the wire-netting, begging without conviction for further illicit supplies. Stephen stooped down automatically and rubbed his nose.

But for the moonlight and the present slackness of the tide the moment was propitious. Stephen walked back more boldly into the conservatory. “You take the feet,” he said.

Without further speech they picked up the bundle and descended laboriously into the garden. The bright moon intimidated John. He looked back over his shoulder for people peering out of windows. But only the windows of his own house commanded the garden; and Mrs. Bantam, his housekeeper, would be long since in bed. Paddling quietly through the dew, he, too, thought fantastically of other burdens he had smuggled down to the river on many a breathless night, pailfuls of potato-peelings and old tins and ashes. In his mind he gave a mute hysterical chuckle at the thought. What other residents, he wondered, had taken this kind of contraband through their gardens in the secret night? Old Dimple, the barrister﻿—ha! ha!﻿—or Mrs. Ambrose? Perhaps they, too, had strangled people in their house and consigned them guiltily to the condoning Thames. Perhaps all those sober, respectable people were capable, like Stephen, of astonishing crimes. Nothing, now, could be really surprising. God, what’s that?

===Chinese===

这会儿没有扑通声，也几乎没有别的声响：只有远处车流焦躁的低语，远处桥上公共汽车偶尔的隆隆，还有鱼儿跳向月亮的小小水声。斯蒂芬近旁，铁丝网围着的兔圈里传来一阵不紧不慢的咀嚼——那是琼的兔子。一只公兔蜷在月光里，津津有味地嚼着晚间卷心菜的最后几片残叶。另一只用鼻子拱着铁丝网，半信半疑地讨要更多违禁的口粮。斯蒂芬下意识地弯腰摸了摸它的鼻子。

若不是有月光、又赶上眼下的平潮，这时机倒是天赐的。斯蒂芬放大胆子走回花房。「你抱脚。」他说。

两人不再多话，抬起包袱，费力地下了花园。明晃晃的月亮让约翰胆怯。他不时回头张望，提防有人从窗口窥探。可俯瞰这座花园的只有他自己家的窗子；而他的管家班塔姆太太（Mrs. Bantam）这会儿早该睡了。他蹚着露水悄悄前行，心里也荒唐地想起另外一些重负——多少个屏住呼吸的夜晚，他往河边偷运过的那些东西：一桶桶土豆皮、旧铁罐和炉灰。想到这里，他在心里发出一声无声的、歇斯底里的窃笑。他纳闷，还有哪些住户曾在隐秘的夜里穿过自家花园运送这路私货？老丁普尔，那位出庭律师——哈！哈！——要不就是安布罗斯太太？说不定他们也在家里掐死过人，然后满怀愧疚地托付给这条宽纵一切的泰晤士河。说不定所有这些古板体面的居民，都跟斯蒂芬一样，干得出惊人的罪行。事到如今，再没什么能真正让人吃惊了。天哪，那是什么？

===Original===

There was a sudden scuffle and clatter in the dark angle by the river wall﻿—only the rabbits panicking into corners at the silent coming of a stranger. But John was aware of the violent beating of his heart.

They laid Emily on the ground and looked over the wall. The tide now had definitely turned. The middle stream was smoothly moving, oily and swift. John felt happier. It would soon be over now. An easy thing, to slip her over into the friendly water﻿ ﻿… no more of this hideous heaving and fumbling with a cold body in a sweat of anxiety.

But to Stephen, regarding doubtfully the close row of boats a hundred yards downstream, new and disquieting uncertainties had occurred. To him, too, it had seemed a simple thing to drop Emily over the wall and let the river dispose of her. But supposing the river failed, flung her against the mooring-chain of one of those boats, jammed her with the tide under the sloping bows of Mr. Adamson’s decrepit hulk, left her there till the tide went down.﻿ ﻿… He saw with a frightening clearness Emily Gaunt being discovered in the morning on the muddy foreshore of Hammerton Terrace﻿—discovered by Andrews, the longshoreman, or a couple of small boys, or Thingummy Rawlins, prowling down from his garden to tinker with his motorboat.﻿ ﻿… No, that would never do.

===Chinese===

河墙边的暗角里突然一阵扑腾乱响——不过是兔子听见生人悄没声地走近，慌慌张张逃进角落罢了。可约翰觉出自己心头狂跳。

他们把埃米莉放在地上，朝墙外望去。潮水这下是真转了向。河心的水顺顺地流动，油亮而湍急。约翰心里松快了些。这就快完事了。把她滑进那友善的水里，多容易的一件事……再不用这样满身焦汗地又搬又弄一具冰凉的尸体，干这些可怕的苦役了。

可对斯蒂芬来说——他正满腹狐疑地打量下游百码开外那一排挨挨挤挤的船——新的、叫人心神不宁的变数又冒了出来。在他看来，本来也是简单的事：把埃米莉翻过墙去，任河水发落。可万一河水不答应，把她甩到哪条船的锚链上，或者让潮水把她塞进亚当森先生（Mr. Adamson）那条破旧大船倾斜的船头底下，撂在那里一直等到退潮。……他骇然清晰地看见：第二天早晨，埃米莉·冈特被人发现在哈默顿台地泥泞的滩涂上——发现她的是码头工人安德鲁斯，或者是两个小男孩，或者是那个叫什么来着的罗林斯（Rawlins）——他正从自家花园溜达下来，摆弄他的摩托艇。……不行，那可使不得。

===Original===

He said in a low voice, “John﻿ ﻿… we’ll have to take her out in the boat﻿ ﻿… we can’t just drop her.﻿ ﻿… These damned boats﻿ ﻿… supposing she caught﻿ ﻿…”

John Egerton uttered a long groan of disappointment. It was not all over, then. There must be more liftings and irritations, more damnable association with this vileness.

“O *Lord*!” he protested. “Stephen, I can’t.﻿ ﻿…” His face was pale and almost piteous under the moon.

===Chinese===

他低声说：「约翰……我们得用船把她送出去……不能就这么一扔了事。……这些该死的船……万一她挂住了……」

约翰·埃格顿发出一声失望的长叹。这么说，还没完。还有得抬、有得气恼，还要跟这桩肮脏事继续纠缠。

「哦，*上帝*！」他哀声抗议。「斯蒂芬，我不行了……」月光下，他的脸苍白得近乎可怜。

===Original===

Stephen answered him without petulance this time. “John, old man﻿—for God’s sake, see it through﻿ ﻿… we *must* get on, and I can’t do it without you.﻿ ﻿… I’m awfully sorry.﻿ ﻿… It’s got to be done.﻿ ﻿…” The appeal in his voice succeeded as an irritable outburst could not have done.

John Egerton braced himself again. In his own mind he recognized the practical wisdom of using the boat. He said with a great weariness, “Come on then.”

It was a long and difficult business getting that body into the boat. A flight of wooden steps led down from the wall to the water, and from there the boat﻿—a small motorboat, half-dinghy, half-canoe﻿—had to be hauled in with a boathook for Stephen to step acrobatically into her and unfasten the moorings. Then she had to be paddled close up under the wall and fastened lightly to the steps. While Stephen was doing this a tug swished by, with a black string of barges clinging clumsily astern. The red eye of her port-light glared banefully across the water. John felt that the man in that tug must guess infallibly what work he was at. A solitary lantern in the stern of the sternmost barge flickered about the single figure standing at the tiller. He could see the face of the man, turned unmistakably towards him.

===Chinese===

这一次斯蒂芬回话没带火气。「约翰，老伙计——看在上帝份上，撑到底……我们*必须*往前赶，没有你我办不成。……实在对不住。……这事躲不掉。……」他声音里那一声恳求奏了效——发脾气可没有这份力量。

约翰·埃格顿重新鼓起劲来。他心里也承认，用船确实是干这事的正路。他带着一身倦意说：「那就走吧。」

把那具尸体弄上船，是桩漫长而费劲的工程。一道木梯从墙头通到水面；得用搭钩把船——一条小汽艇，半像小划艇，半像独木舟——钩到梯边，好让斯蒂芬一个鹞子翻身跳上去，解开系缆。然后还得把船划到墙根下，轻轻系在梯级上。斯蒂芬正忙这些的时候，一艘拖船唰地驶过，船尾笨拙地拖着一条黑色的驳船串。它左舷灯的红眼睛恶狠狠地瞪过水面。约翰觉得，那拖船上的人必定一猜一个准，猜出他在干什么活计。最末那条驳船的船尾挑着一盏孤零零的风灯，照着掌舵人孤单的身影，一晃一晃。他看得见那张脸，明明白白正朝着他这边。

===Original===

She was travelling fast, and Stephen cursed as her wash took hold of his little boat and tossed her up and banged her against the wall and the rickety steps. John, leaning anxiously over, could hear his muttered execrations as he fended her off.

Then there was a hot, whispered argument﻿—on the best way of getting the body down, Stephen standing swaying in the boat, with his face upturned, like some ridiculous moonlight lover, John flinging down assertions and reasonings in a forced whisper which broke now and then into a harsh undertone. Stephen thought it should be carted down the steps. John, with an aching objection to further prolonged contact with the thing, said it should be lowered with a rope. “Haven’t you a bit of rope?” he reiterated﻿—“a bit of rope﻿—much the best.”

Sick of argument, Stephen fumbled with wild mutterings in his locker, and brought out in a muddle of oilcans and tools a length of stout cord. Together they made a rough bight about Emily’s middle, together lifted her to the flat stone parapet of the wall.

===Chinese===

那拖船开得飞快，尾流一把攥住斯蒂芬的小船，把它抛起来，砰砰地撞在墙上和摇摇晃晃的木梯上，斯蒂芬骂声不绝。约翰焦急地俯下身，听得见他挡船时压着嗓门的咒骂。

接着是一场火药味十足的耳语争吵——争的是怎么把尸体弄下墙去：斯蒂芬站在船里摇摇晃晃，仰着一张脸，活像月光下哪个滑稽的痴情人；约翰强压着嗓子往下甩出一条条论断和道理，时不时破成粗哑的低音。斯蒂芬认为该顺着台阶拖下去。约翰一想到还要跟这东西再长时间贴身接触就痛楚难当，主张用绳子吊下去。「你就没有一截绳子？」他翻来覆去地问——「一截绳子——这个最稳妥。」

斯蒂芬吵得不耐烦了，嘴里胡乱咕哝着，在贮物柜里一通乱摸，从油壶和工具堆里拖出一截结实的粗绳。两人合力在埃米莉腰上系了个粗略的绳圈，又合力把她抬上矮墙平整的石栏。

===Original===

When she was there a dog barked suspiciously in Hammerton Terrace; another echoed him along The Chase. The two men crouched against the wall in a tense and ridiculous agitation.

Through all these emergencies and arguments and muffled objurgations there stirred in John’s mind ironical recollections of passages in detective stories, where dead bodies were constantly being transported with facility and dispatch in any desired direction. It seemed so easy in the books, it was so damnably difficult in practice﻿—or so they were finding it.

And always there was the menace of Margery’s return; she must be back soon, she would certainly come out into the garden on a night like this.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她一上去，哈默顿台地那边一条狗疑心地吠了起来；蔡斯街上又有一条遥相应和。两个人贴着墙根蹲下，紧张得可笑，慌乱得也可笑。

在这一连串的险情、争执和压低的咒骂声中，约翰的脑子里翻搅起对侦探小说里种种桥段的讽刺回忆：书里的尸体运起来总是轻而易举、雷厉风行，要送哪个方向就送哪个方向。书里看着那么容易，实践起来却难得要命——反正他们俩正领教着。

而玛杰丽回来的威胁始终悬在头顶；她很快就要到家了，这样的夜晚，她准会到花园里来走走。……

===Original===

When they had the body stretched flat and ready on the wall, Stephen went back into the boat. It had sidled down below the steps, and had to be hauled back. The tide was maddeningly strong. Stephen urged the boat with imprecations under the wall. To keep it there he must hold on stoutly with a boathook, and could give little help to John in the detested task of lowering the sack. John’s hands were clammy with sweat like the hands of a gross man. He gripped the rope with a desperate energy and thrust Emily gently over the side. The rope dragged and scraped across the parapet; the body swayed in the moonlight with a preposterous seesaw motion. When it was halfway to the water, they heard a tug puffing rhythmically towards them﻿—somewhere beyond the Island. It was not yet in sight, but a resistless unreasoning panic immediately invaded them. Stephen, with one free hand, clawed recklessly at an edge of sacking; John, in a furious effort to quicken the descent of Emily, lost altogether his control of the rope. The rope slipped swiftly through his moist and impotent palms. Emily, with an intimidating bump and a wooden clatter of sculls, fell ponderously into the boat and lay sprawled across the gunwale. A sibilant “Damned fool!” slid up the wall from Stephen, almost overbalanced by the sudden descent of the body. The two men waited with an elaborate assumption of innocence while the tug fussed past, their hearts pounding absurdly. Then, before the wash had come, John Egerton stepped gingerly down the creaking steps, and they pushed out into the rolling reflection of the moon. The nose of the boat lifted steeply on the oily swell of the tug’s wash, and the head of Emily slipped down with a thump over the thwart, her feet still projecting obliquely over the side; John Egerton pulled them in. He looked back with a new disquiet at the still and silvery houses of Hammerton Terrace, at the dim shrubberies along The Chase. There were lights in some of the houses. Out there under the public moon he felt very visible and suspect﻿—a naked feeling.

He heard a remote mutter from Stephen, paddling in the bows: “Too many of these damned tugs!” and another: “This filthy *moon*!” They were working slowly against the tide between the Island and the mainland of The Chase. Stephen’s plan was to round the top of the Island, cross the river, and get rid of Emily in the shadows of the other side, drifting down with the tide.

Even in the narrow channel by the bank the tide was exasperating, and paddling the boat, heavy with the engine, was slow work and strenuous. But the engine would be too noisy. And it was an uncertain starter.

===Chinese===

等尸体在墙上放平、一切就绪，斯蒂芬回到船上。船早已自己溜到木梯底下去了，只好再钩回来。潮水大得逼人发疯。斯蒂芬骂骂咧咧地把船逼到墙根下。要稳住船，他就得用搭钩死死撑住，帮不上约翰什么忙——下放口袋这桩他深恶痛绝的活计。约翰双手满是黏汗，像个大胖子的手。他拼死攥住绳子，轻轻把埃米莉送出墙沿。绳子在石栏上拖一阵磨一阵；尸体在月光里晃晃悠悠，一翘一翘，荒唐透顶。落到一半，他们听见一艘拖船突突突、有板有眼地朝他们驶来——在河洲那头的什么地方。还看不见影，可一股不由分说、毫无道理的恐慌立刻把他们淹没了。斯蒂芬腾出一只手，不管不顾地去抓口袋的一角；约翰急红了眼，只想让埃米莉快些降下去，手上的绳子彻底失了控。绳子从他潮湿而绵软无力的掌心里飞快地溜走。埃米莉携着一声唬人的闷响、伴着木桨哗啦啦一串响，沉甸甸砸进船里，横摊在船舷上。斯蒂芬被这陡然的下坠带得几乎失去平衡，一声咝咝的「该死的蠢货！」贴着墙根溜了上来。两人煞费苦心地装出一脸清白，等那拖船咋咋呼呼驶过去，心荒唐地狂跳。随后，赶在尾浪到来之前，约翰·埃格顿蹑手蹑脚踩下吱呀作响的木梯，两人把船撑进月亮翻滚的倒影里。拖船尾浪油亮的涌把船头高高抬起，埃米莉的头咚的一声滑过座板，两只脚还斜翘在船舷外；约翰·埃格顿把它们拽了进来。他带着新的不安回头望去：哈默顿台地银白而静谧的房舍，蔡斯沿线影影绰绰的树丛。有几户人家还亮着灯。在这轮大庭广众的月亮底下，他觉得自己显眼极了、可疑极了——一种赤身露体的感觉。

他听见斯蒂芬在船头划桨，远远地咕哝：「这些该死的拖船也太多了！」又一句：「这倒霉的*月亮*！」他们正沿河洲和蔡斯本岸之间的水道，缓缓逆潮而行。斯蒂芬的计划是绕过河洲上端，横渡河面，把埃米莉丢进对岸的阴影里，再随潮水漂下来。

即便在贴岸的窄水道里，潮水也恼人得很；用桨划这条驮着发动机的笨船，又慢又费劲。可发动机会太吵。再说，它起动起来没个准。

===Original===

Stephen said at last, “Hell! get out the sculls!”

John Egerton groped in the locker for rowlocks with an oppressive sense of incompetence and delay. His fingers moved with an ineffectual urgency in a messy confusion of spanners and oilcans, tins of grease, and slimy labyrinths of thin cord. Only one rowlock was discoverable. The finding of the second became in his mind a task of inconceivable importance and difficulty. Vast issues depended on it﻿—Stephen﻿ ﻿… Margery﻿ ﻿… babies﻿ ﻿… Emily Gaunt﻿ ﻿… and somehow or other Mrs. Bantam. Thunderous mutterings rolled down distantly from the bows. John groaned helplessly. He caught his fingers sharply on the edge of a screwdriver. “It’s not here﻿ ﻿… it’s not here﻿ ﻿… it *can’t* be, Stephen.” With a sense of heroic measures he hauled out in clattering handfuls the whole muddle of implements in the locker. Under the electric coil lurked the missing rowlock.

“Row, then, like the devil,” ordered Stephen. Out here, in this strange watery adventure, Stephen was the readily acknowledged commander. John rowed, with grunts and splashings.

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬终于开了口：「见鬼！把桨拿出来！」

约翰·埃格顿在贮物柜里摸索桨架，心头沉甸甸地觉得自己无能、误事。他的手指带着徒劳的急切，在扳手、油壶、油脂罐和一团团黏滑细绳的迷宫里乱翻。只找到一只桨架。找第二只，在他心里成了一桩重要和艰难得不可想象的差事。多少重大的干系悬在上面——斯蒂芬……玛杰丽……孩子……埃米莉·冈特……也不知怎么的，还有班塔姆太太。雷鸣般的咕哝从船头远远滚下来。约翰无助地呻吟。手指让螺丝刀的棱狠狠割了一下。「不在这儿……不在这儿……*不可能*不在这儿啊，斯蒂芬。」他抱着壮士断腕的决心，哗啦啦几大把，把贮物柜里那一团乱糟糟的家什全拖了出来。电气线圈底下，趴着那只失踪的桨架。

「那就划吧，往死里划。」斯蒂芬下令。在这场离奇的水上冒险里，斯蒂芬是众所公认的统帅。约翰划起桨来，呼哧带喘，水花四溅。

===Original===

They rounded the Island, the moon glowing remotely beyond it through the traceries of young willow stems. Stephen was doing something with an anchor at the mouth of the sack, breathing audibly through his nose. John sculled obliquely across the river, struggling against the tide, steadily losing ground, he felt. “Losing ground,” he thought insanely, “ought to be losing *water*, of course.” So strangely do the minds of men move in critical hours.

When they were halfway over, the chunk-chunk of a motorboat came lazily upstream. “God!” said Stephen, “a police-boat.” John thought, “Will it *never* end?” It was appalling, this accumulation of obstacles and delays and potential witnesses. He was tired now, and acutely conscious of a general perspiration.

They drifted downstream under the bank, while the police-boat phutted up on the far side, a low black shape without lights. Caped figures chattered easily in the stern and took no evident notice of the small white motorboat under the bank; but Stephen and John imagined fatal suspicions and perceptions proceeding under the peaked caps. They passed.

===Chinese===

他们绕过河洲，月亮在洲那头远远地亮着，透过幼柳枝条的细网洒下光来。斯蒂芬正在袋口那儿摆弄一只锚，鼻息声听得清清楚楚。约翰斜着往对岸摇桨，跟潮水较劲，只觉得船一个劲儿地吃亏。「吃亏，」他痴痴地想，「照理说，在这儿该是*吃水*才对。」人的头脑在危急关头就是这样奇怪地运转。

船到河心一半，突突突的马达声懒洋洋地从上游过来。「天哪！」斯蒂芬说，「警察巡逻艇。」约翰想：「这*到底*有没有个完？」障碍、耽搁、潜在的目击者，这样层层叠叠地堆上来，实在骇人。他这会儿累了，浑身的汗，每一寸皮肤都觉出来。

他们贴着本岸往下游漂，巡逻艇在对面突突突地上行，一个低矮的黑影，不点灯。披斗篷的人影坐在船尾轻松地闲聊，显然没把岸边那条小小的白汽艇放在眼里；可斯蒂芬和约翰认定，那几顶大盖帽底下正进行着致命的怀疑和洞察。他们错过去了。

===Original===

“*Now!*” Stephen was fiddling with his anchor again, tugging at a knot; his tone was final. “Take her out into the middle again﻿ ﻿… *quick*!”

John pulled gallantly with his left. They were opposite the house again now, moving smoothly towards Hammersmith Bridge. No other craft was in sight or sound.

Stephen said thickly, “If we don’t get her over now, we never shall﻿ ﻿… stand by.﻿ ﻿… No, no﻿ ﻿… you trim the boat.﻿ ﻿… I’ll manage it.”

===Chinese===

「*现在！*」斯蒂芬又在摆弄他的锚，拽着一个绳结；语气斩钉截铁。「再把船划到河心……*快*！」

约翰勇猛地用左手划桨。此刻他们又正对着那所房子了，正平稳地朝哈默史密斯桥滑去。视野里、听觉里，都没有别的船只。

斯蒂芬口齿含混地说：「这回再不把她送下去，就永远送不下去了……各就各位。……不，不……你稳住船。……我来弄。」

===Original===

He edged Emily close up against the gunwale, her extremities on a couple of thwarts, her middle sagging down the side of the boat. He looked quickly up the river and down the river and at Hammerton Terrace and at the oil-mills below and at the empty towpath on the opposite bank, all silent, all still. Stephen put a hand under the sack. Close by a tiny fish leaped lightly from the river. Stephen saw the flash of its belly, and took his hand away with a start. Then with a great heave under Emily’s middle, a violent pushing and lifting with feet and body and arms, that set the sculls clattering and the boat precariously rocking he got the body half over the gunwale, John perched anxiously on the other side, striving to correct the already dangerous list. Stephen struggled blasphemously with the infuriating sack. Somehow, somewhere it was maddeningly entangled with something in the boat. Frantic tugging and thrusting, irritable oaths, moved it not at all. John looked fearfully behind him. A lighted omnibus was swimming through space, perilously near﻿ ﻿… Hammersmith Bridge. Stephen was kicking the body now with a futile savagery.

“What the hell?” he said. “O God!”

John groped distantly with a hand in the dark. Then, “The anchor!” he said﻿—“the anchor’s caught.﻿ ﻿…” He heard a relieved “O Lord!” from Stephen, “thought I’d put the anchor end over first”﻿—and for the first time made himself a petulant comment, “Why the devil didn’t you?” It was too much﻿—this sort of thing. Then the shaggy end of the sack was slithering quietly over the side, the anchor twinkled swiftly in the moon, and the relieved boat rocked suddenly with a wild, delighted levity. Emily was gone.

===Chinese===

他把埃米莉挪到紧贴船舷的地方，头脚担在两张座板上，腰身顺着船帮往下坠。他飞快地朝河上游看一眼、下游看一眼，看看哈默顿台地，看看下游的榨油坊，再看看对岸空无一人的纤道——全都悄无声息，全都纹丝不动。斯蒂芬把手伸到口袋底下。近旁一条小鱼轻轻跃出河面。斯蒂芬瞥见那鱼腹一闪，一惊，缩回了手。随即他把全身的劲灌到埃米莉的腰下，脚、身子、胳膊一齐猛推猛举，震得双桨咔啦作响、小船摇摇欲坠，总算把尸身推过了半个船舷；约翰在另一边提心吊胆地悬着，拼命想压住船身那已经危险的倾斜。斯蒂芬一边声声咒骂着神明，一边跟这个气死人的口袋搏斗。不知怎么、也不知在哪儿，它跟船上的什么东西死死缠住了。发疯似的连拽带捅，加上烦躁的咒骂，它纹丝不动。约翰胆战心惊地回头望去。一辆亮着灯的公共汽车正游在半空，近得吓人……哈默史密斯桥。斯蒂芬这会儿正抬脚踹那具尸体，踹得徒劳而凶狠。

「搞什么名堂？」他说。「哦，上帝！」

约翰恍恍惚惚地伸手到黑暗里摸索。然后：「是锚！」他说——「锚挂住了。……」他听见斯蒂芬如释重负的一声「哦，老天！」「我还当是我先放了带锚的那一头」——他头一回冒出一句气鼓鼓的抢白：「那你他妈干吗不先放？」实在忍无可忍——这种事。随后，口袋毛茸茸的那一头悄悄滑过船舷，锚在月光里倏地一闪，卸了重负的小船猛地一晃，晃出一种狂野的、欢天喜地的轻快。埃米莉没了。

===Original===

Peering back upstream, the two men saw a slowly expanding circle on the black water. And there were a few bubbles. Emily was indeed gone.

Stephen sat in a limp posture of absolute exhaustion, his shoulders hunched, his head on his hands, speechless.

John looked at his watch. It was a quarter-past ten﻿—only about an hour since Emily died. He stared incredulous at the faintly luminous hands. Then he looked round; the boat seemed to be drifting very fast. On his right were the boathouses, a dark huddle of boats clinging to the rafts in front of them. The boathouses were next to the Bridge.

===Chinese===

两人朝上游回望，看见黑色的水面上一个缓缓扩大的圆圈。还有几个气泡。埃米莉是真的没了。

斯蒂芬瘫坐在那里，彻底力竭，肩膀高高耸起，头伏在双手上，一言不发。

约翰看了看表。十点一刻——埃米莉断气才不过一个钟头光景。他难以置信地盯着那两根微微发光的指针。随后他环顾四周；船好像漂得飞快。右边是船库，黑压压一片船挤在库前的筏子旁。船库就在大桥旁边。

===Original===

He looked back and up, with a new fear. The long span of the suspension bridge hung almost above them. A bus rumbled ominously above. Two persons were standing on the footpath against the parapet, looking down at the boat. He could see the pale blobs of their faces. One of them had a Panama hat.

The boat shot into the dark under the Bridge.

John leaned forward. “Stephen,” he whispered﻿—“Stephen.” There was no answer. John touched his knee. “Stephen.”

===Chinese===

他回过头，仰起脸，带着一种新的恐惧。吊桥的长跨几乎就悬在他们头顶。一辆公共汽车在头顶隆隆驶过，凶多吉少。两个人站在人行道上，倚着桥栏，正低头看这条船。他看得见那两团发白的脸影。其中一个戴一顶巴拿马草帽。

船一头扎进桥下的黑暗里。

约翰俯身向前。「斯蒂芬，」他低声唤——「斯蒂芬。」没有回应。约翰碰碰他的膝盖。「斯蒂芬。」

===Original===

A yellow face lifted slowly. “What is it?”

“There was someone watching on the Bridge﻿ ﻿… two men.”

Stephen sighed with a profound weariness.

“It can’t be helped,” he said.

===Chinese===

一张蜡黄的脸缓缓抬起来。「怎么了？」

「桥上有人在看……两个男的。」

斯蒂芬叹了口气，倦意深重。

「没办法的事。」他说。

===Original===

A dreadful paralysis seemed to have succeeded the heavy strain. He looked as the men used to look after a long spell in the line, sitting at last in a dingy billet﻿—played out.

John Egerton took the sculls and turned the boat round. The boat moved stiffly, with a steady gurgle at the bows; the noiseless tide swung violently by; the oars creaked complainingly.

“This *tide*﻿ ﻿…” muttered John.

Stephen Byrne raised his head. “The tide’s going out,” he said stupidly.

===Chinese===

一场骇人的麻痹，似乎接替了方才那重压般的紧张。他那副模样，就像从前在前线苦守久战的人，终于坐进一间昏暗的营房——彻底垮了。

约翰·埃格顿拿起双桨，把船掉过头。船走得滞重，船头汩汩直响；无声的潮水从旁边猛烈地荡过；船桨吱吱呀呀，满腹怨言。

「这*潮水*……」约翰咕哝。

斯蒂芬·伯恩抬起头来。「潮在退。」他呆呆地说。

## IV / 四

===Original===

Margery Byrne walked home very happily from the Underground Station at Stamford Brook, The ticket collector uttered a reverent “Good night, mum”; the policeman at the corner of St. Peter’s Square brightened suddenly at her and saluted with the imperishable manner of past military service. The world was very kind and friendly, she felt. But that was the usual manner of the world to Margery Byrne. The world invariably looked at her as it passed her in the street. The male world invariably looked again. The mannerless male world usually looked back. The shameless male world stared at her in Tubes and manoeuvred obviously for commanding positions. But that part of the world, having secured its positions, was generally either disappointed or abashed. There was an aspect of fragility and virtue about her which stirred in the bold and shameless male the almost atrophied instincts of chivalry and protection. After a little they ceased to stare, but opened doors for her with a conscious knighthood. There are women who make a man feel evil at the sight of them. Margery made a man feel good.

But this aspect of fragility was without any suggestion of feebleness. It was just that she was slight and fair, and her face small and her features intensely delicate and refined. She had a rarefied look﻿—as if all flaws and imperfections and superfluities had been somehow chemically removed, leaving only the essential stamina and grace. For she had stamina. She walked with an easy un-urban swing, and she could walk a long way. Her lips were little and slightly anaemic, but firm. There was an evident will in the determined and perfectly proportioned chin. The nose was small but admirably straight and set very close above the mouth. Only her large blue eyes seemed a little out of proportion, but these suggested a warm sympathy which the smallness of her features might otherwise have concealed. Her head, balanced attractively on straight white shoulders, was covered gloriously, if a little thinly, with hair of a light gold, an indescribable tint not often encountered outside the world of books. But such, in fact, was Margery’s hair. Her skin also was of a colour and texture not to be painted in words﻿—it had that indefinable quality for which there has been discovered no better name than transparent. And this pale, almost colourless quality of complexion completed the effect of fragility, of physical refinement.

It was still and sultry in St. Peter’s Square. The old moon hung above the church and lit up the ridiculous stone eagles on the decayed and pompous houses on Margery’s right. “Like lecterns,” she thought, for the thousandth time.

===Chinese===

玛杰丽·伯恩从斯坦福德布鲁克（Stamford Brook）地铁站走回家去，一路欢天喜气。收票员恭恭敬敬地道了声「晚安，太太」；圣彼得广场（St. Peter’s Square）拐角上的警察见了她，顿时精神一振，行了个礼——往日行伍出身留下的做派，一辈子也磨灭不掉。她觉得，这世界真是和善而友好。不过，世人待玛杰丽·伯恩，向来就是这个样子。她走在街上，世人无一例外都要看她一眼。男性世人无一例外还要再看一眼。没教养的男性世人通常会回过头去看。不要脸的男性世人则在地铁车厢里死盯着她，还明目张胆地抢占有利地形。可是这部分世人一旦占好了位置，多半不是大失所望，就是自惭形秽。她身上有一种纤弱而贞静的气质，能搅动大胆不要脸的男人心里那近乎退化了的本能——骑士精神，和保护欲。过了一小会儿，他们便不再盯着她了，而是带着几分自觉的骑士身份，替她开门。世上有一种女人，男人一见就觉得自己坏。玛杰丽却让男人一见就觉得自己好。

不过这种纤弱，丝毫不含虚弱的意味。她只是身量纤细、肤色白皙，脸庞小巧，五官精致清雅到了极处。她周身有一种滤净了的气质——仿佛一切瑕疵、缺陷与赘余，都经某种化学方法提除了，只剩下最本质的元气与风韵。因为她有的是元气。她走起路来步态舒展洒脱，全无城里人的拘谨，而且很能走远路。她的嘴唇小小的，略微带点贫血似的苍白，却很坚定。那下巴端正匀称、比例无可挑剔，透着一股显见的意志。鼻子小巧，却挺直得可羡，紧紧挨在嘴巴上方。唯独那双蓝色大眼睛，跟五官比起来似乎略大了些，可正是它们透出一种温暖的同情——若非如此，五官的纤小反倒会把这份同情掩住。她的头端端正正、惹人注目地安在笔直白皙的肩上，覆着一头浅金色的头发，华光四射，虽则略嫌稀薄——那是一种难以描摹的色调，书本以外的世界里不常见到。可玛杰丽的头发偏偏就是这种颜色。她的皮肤，那色泽质地同样不是文字描画得出的——它有一种说不清的品质，人们至今没有找出比「透明」更好的名字来称呼它。而这苍白得近乎无色的容颜，更成全了那一身纤弱、那一种身体上的精致。

圣彼得广场上闷热无风。残月悬在教堂上空，照亮了玛杰丽右手边那些破败而浮华的宅子上一只只可笑的石鹰。「真像读经台。」她想——这已是第一千次了。

===Original===

The houses were square and semidetached, two in one; a life-size eagle perched over every porch, its neck screwed tragically towards its sister-eagle craning sympathetically on the neighbouring porch, seeking apparently forever a never-to-be-attained communion. What sort of people lived there, Margery wondered, and why? So far from town and no view of the river, no special attraction. The people of The Chase always wondered in this way as they walked through St. Peter’s Square. The problems of who lived in it and why were permanently insoluble since nobody who lived in The Chase knew anybody who lived in the Square. They knew each other, and that was enough. They knew it was worth while travelling a long way if you lived in The Chase, because of the river, the views, the openness, and the fine old rambling, rickety houses. But why should anyone live in an inland square with eagles over the front doors?

Margery did not know. And she had other things to think of. Tomorrow she must speak seriously to Emily. Emily, like all these young women, had started excellently, but was becoming slack. And impertinent, sometimes. But one must be careful. Just now was not the time to frighten her away. Then Trueman’s man was coming for the curtains in the morning; they must be got ready. And there was a mountain of needlework to be done. And she must run through Stephen’s clothes again﻿—before she was too ill for it. Only a month more now, perhaps less. That was a blessing. She was not frightened this time﻿—not like the first time, with little Joan﻿—that *had* been rather terrifying﻿—not knowing quite what it was like. But it was a long, interminable business; for such ages, it seemed, you had to “be careful,” not play tennis, or go out to dinner just when you wanted to. You felt a fool sometimes, inventing reasons for not doing things, when of course there was only one reason. And so ugly﻿—especially in London﻿ ﻿… going about in shops﻿ ﻿… and Tubes.

Never mind. It was worth it. And afterwards.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

这些房子方方正正，全是两宅合一的半独立式；每家门廊顶上蹲着一只与真人一般大的石鹰，脖颈悲惨地拧向邻居家门廊上那只同样伸长了脖子、满脸同情的姊妹鹰，看来要求远寻求一种永无着落的相通。住在那里面的是些什么人，又图什么呢，玛杰丽想不明白。离城这么远，望不见河，也没什么特别可取的地方。宅区的人每次穿过圣彼得广场，都要这么纳闷一番。谁住在里面、为什么住在里面，这两大难题永远无解，因为住在宅区的人，谁也不认识住在广场里的人。他们彼此认识，这就够了。他们知道，住进了宅区，路远些也值得，因为有河，有景致，有开阔的天地，还有那些曲折蔓延、摇摇欲坠的漂亮老房子。可是，为什么偏偏有人要住在一个大门顶上蹲着鹰的内陆广场上呢？

玛杰丽不知道。她还有别的心事要想。明天她得跟埃米莉严肃地谈一谈。埃米莉跟所有这些年轻姑娘一样，起头起得好极了，如今却渐渐懈怠下来。有时候还冒失无礼。可得小心着点儿。眼下正不是把她吓跑的时候。还有，明天上午特鲁曼（Trueman）店里的人要来取窗帘，得先把它们拾掇妥当。还有堆成山的针线活等着做。还有斯蒂芬的衣服，她也得再翻检一遍——趁她还没病得动不了。只剩一个月了，说不定还不到。这真是福气。这一回她不怕了——不像头一回生小琼那阵子——那一回*确实*够吓人的——还不大清楚那究竟是怎么一回事。可这桩事情实在漫长，没完没了；仿佛有大段大段的时光，你都得「处处当心」，不能打网球，想出去吃饭也偏去不成。有时候自觉傻透了，编出一堆不去做事的理由，其实理由当然只有一个。而且人变得那么难看——尤其是在伦敦……在铺子里走来走去……还有地铁里。

不要紧。这是值得的。再说，还有往后呢。……

===Original===

Margery cast her mind deliciously forward to that “afterwards.” They would all go away somewhere, her dear Stephen and Joan and a new and adorable little Stephen. She was determined that it should be a boy this time. That was what Stephen wanted, and what he wanted, within reason, he should have. He deserved it, the dear man. Really, he was becoming an amazingly perfect husband. Becoming, yes﻿—for just at first he had been difficult. But that was during the war; they had seen so little of each other﻿—and he was always worried, overworked. But now they had really “settled down,” the horrid war was done with, and he had been too wonderfully delightful and nice to her. Lately especially. Much more considerate and helpful and﻿—and, yes, demonstrative. She felt more sure of him. She was appalled, sometimes, to think how essential he was to her, how frightfully dependent she had become on the existence of this one man, met quite by chance, or what was called chance, at somebody else’s house. If anything should happen now﻿—Even the children would be a poor consolation.

But nothing would happen. He would go on being more and more delicious and successful; she would go on being happy and proud, watching eagerly the maturement of her ambitions for him. Even now she was intensely proud of him﻿—though, of course, it would never do to let him suspect it.

It was an astounding thing, this literary triumph. Secretly, she admitted, she had never had enormous faith in his poetical powers. She had liked his work because it was his. And being the daughter of a mildly literary man, she had developed a serious critical faculty capable of generously appraising any artistic effort of real sincerity and promise. But she had seldom thought of Stephen’s poetry in terms of the market, of public favour and material reward. Certainly she had not married him as “a poet” or even “a writer.” But that only made his meteoric success more dazzling and delightful. Sometimes it was almost impossible to realize, she found, that this young man she had married was the same Stephen Byrne whose name was everywhere﻿—on the bookstalls, in the publishers’ advertisements, in literary articles in any paper you picked up; that all over the country men and women were buying and reading and rereading and quoting and discussing bits of poetry which *her* husband had scribbled down on odd bits of paper at her own house. It was astounding. Margery was passing the small houses at the end of the Square, the homes of clerks and shop-people and superior artisans. She glanced at a group of wives, garrulously taking the air at a doorway, and almost pitied them because *their* husbands’ names were never before the public. It seemed awful, now, to be absolutely obscure.

===Chinese===

玛杰丽美滋滋地把心思投向那个「往后」。到时候他们全家要出门去什么地方住上一阵：她亲爱的斯蒂芬、琼，还有一个新添的、可爱得不得了的小斯蒂芬。她拿定了主意，这一回准得是个男孩。那正是斯蒂芬想要的，而他想要的，只要不离谱，就该让他得到。他配得上，这个亲爱的人。说真的，他正变成一个完美得惊人的丈夫。说「正变成」——不错——因为起初那阵子他实在难处。可那是在大战期间；两人聚少离多——他又总是忧心、劳累过度。可如今他们总算真正「安顿下来」了，可恶的战争完事了，而他待她好得不得了，亲热体贴极了。近来尤其如此。体贴多了，肯搭手多了，还有——还有，对了，热乎多了。她觉得他更靠得住了。她有时候想起来会吓一跳：他对她竟这样要紧，她对这一个人的存在竟依赖到这般地步——当初还是在别人家里遇见他的，纯属偶然，或者说，所谓偶然。这会儿要是出上什么事——就算有孩子，也算不得多大的安慰。

可是什么也不会发生。他会一直这么可爱下去、有出息下去；她会一直这么幸福而骄傲，热切地望着自己为他怀抱的种种雄心一天天长成。即便此刻，她已经为他骄傲得不得了——当然，万万不能让他察觉。

这桩文学的胜利，实在叫人惊叹。她私下里承认，对于他的诗才，她从来没抱过太大的信心。她喜欢他的作品，只因那是他的。而身为一个多少沾些文气的男人家的女儿，她养成了一副正经的批评眼光，足以宽厚地鉴定任何真正有诚意、有前途的艺术努力。可她很少拿市场、拿公众的宠爱和物质的报偿这类尺度，去衡量斯蒂芬的诗。当然，她嫁他的时候，并不是把他当「诗人」嫁的，连「作家」都不是。可这样一来，他流星似的成功反倒愈发炫目、愈发叫人欢喜了。她发现，有时候简直没法子认识到：她嫁的这个年轻人，就是那个名字无处不在的斯蒂芬·伯恩——书报摊上、出版商的广告里、随手抄起的哪份报纸的文学版上；全英国的男男女女，都在买、在读、在读第二遍、在引用、在议论那些诗行，而那是*她*丈夫在她自己家里、涂在零零碎碎的纸片上的。真是不可思议。玛杰丽正走到广场尽头那些小房子跟前，住的是职员、店员和高一档的手艺人。她瞥见门口一群太太叽叽喳喳地乘凉，几乎要可怜起她们来——*她们*丈夫的名字，永远不会出现在公众眼前。如今想来，彻底地默默无闻，实在可怕。

===Original===

No. She didn’t think that really. After all, it was an “extra,” this fame. It had nothing to do with her marrying Stephen; it would have nothing to do with her happiness with Stephen. It was a kind of matrimonial windfall. What really mattered was Stephen himself, and Margery herself, and the way in which they fitted together. What, she really﻿—yes, *adored*﻿—there was no other word﻿—was himself, his black hair and his twinkling smile, his laugh and jolliness and funny little ways. And his character. That, of course, was the foundation of it all. A dear and excellent character. Other men, even the best of them, did horrid things sometimes. Stephen, she knew, with all his faults﻿—a little selfish, perhaps﻿—conceited? no, but self-centred, rather﻿—would never do anything mean or degrading or treacherous. She could trust him absolutely. He would certainly never disgrace her as some men did disgrace their wives﻿—women, drink, and so on. “The soul of honour”﻿—that was the phrase.﻿ ﻿… That, again, was a marvellous piece of fortune, that out of a world of peccant questionable men she should have been allowed to appropriate a man like Stephen, so nearly perfect and secure. No wonder she had this consuming, this frightening sense of adoration, sometimes. But she tried to suppress that. It was dangerous. “Thou shalt not bow down﻿ ﻿…”

Margery smiled secretly and turned her latchkey in the lock.

In the hall she noticed immediately Stephen’s hat on the peg, and was glad that he was home. She walked through with her letters to the garden, and looked out over the wall. The boat was gone, and she was faintly disappointed. Far down the river she fancied she saw it, a dirty whiteness, and resisted an impulse to call to Stephen. It must be nice on the river tonight. The rabbits rustled stealthily in the corner; a faint unpleasant smell hung about their home. She looked absently at the rabbit Paul, his nose twitching endlessly in the moonlight, and went in to bed.

===Chinese===

不。她心里并不是真这么想。说到底，这名声是笔「外快」。当初她嫁斯蒂芬，与它不相干；往后她跟斯蒂芬的幸福，也与它不相干。这不过是婚姻里的一注意外之财。真正要紧的是斯蒂芬本人，是玛杰丽本人，是他们俩凑在一处的合适法子。她真正——对，*爱慕*——再没有别的字眼——的是他本人：他的黑头发，他忽闪忽闪的笑，他的笑声、他的快活劲儿、他那些好笑的小习惯。还有他的品性。那当然是一切的根基。一副亲爱而出色的品性。别的男人，连最好的那些，有时候也会干出混账事来。斯蒂芬呢，她知道，尽管有种种毛病——有点儿自私，也许——自负么？不，毋宁说自我为中心——却绝不会干出任何卑鄙、下作、背信的事来。她可以绝对地信任他。他肯定永远不会像有些男人亏待妻子那样亏待她——女人啦，喝酒啦，那一套。「荣誉的化身」——说的就是这种人。……这又是一桩了不得的运气：满世界都是德行有亏、靠不住的男人，她居然获准把斯蒂芬这样一个几乎完美而稳妥的男人据为私有。难怪她有时候会涌起这种灼人的、这种叫人害怕的爱慕之情。可她竭力把它压下去。这很危险。「不可跪拜……」

玛杰丽偷偷一笑，把大门钥匙转进了锁眼。

进了门厅，她一眼就看见挂钩上斯蒂芬的帽子，很高兴他到家了。她拿着信一路走到花园里，隔墙朝外望。船不在了，她心里微微有点失望。老远的河面上，她仿佛望见了它，一小团发暗的白，便忍住了唤斯蒂芬一声的冲动。今晚河上一定很惬意。兔子们在墙角窸窸窣窣；它们窝边挂着一股淡淡的怪味。她心不在焉地望了望兔子保罗——月光底下，它的鼻子一抽一抽没个停——便进屋睡觉去了。

===Original===

When she had undressed she leaned for a long time out of the high window looking at the night. Across the river lay the broad reservoirs of the water company, and the first houses were half a mile away; so that from the window on a night like this you looked over seemingly endless stretches of gleaming water; strangers coming there at nighttime wondered at the wide spaciousness of this obscure corner of London. You could imagine yourself easily in some Oriental city. Hammersmith and Chiswick and Barnes wore a romantic coat of shadow and silver. The carved reflections of the small trees on the other bank were so nearly like reflected rows of palms. The far-off outline of factories against the sky had the awe and mystery of mosques. In the remote murmur of London traffic there was the note, at once lazy and sinister, treacherous and reposeful, of an Eastern town. And now when no tugs went by and nothing stirred, the silent river, rushing smoothly into the black heart of London, had for Margery something of the sombre majesty of the Nile, hinting at dark unnameable things, passion and death and furtive cruelties, and all that sense of secrecy and crime which clings to the riverside of great cities, the world over.

Margery wondered idly how much of all that talk about the Thames was true; whether horrible things were still done secretly beside her beloved river, hidden and condoned by the river, carried away to the sea.﻿ ﻿… Down in the docks, no doubt.﻿ ﻿… Wapping and so on.

The prosaic thumping of a tug broke the spell of Margery’s imagination. She looked up and down for Stephen’s boat, a faint crossness in her mind because of his lateness. She got into bed. She was sleepy, but she would read and doze a little till he came in.

===Chinese===

她宽衣之后，久久地凭倚在高窗上望着夜色。河对岸铺展着自来水公司宽宽的水库，最近的人家也在半英里之外；于是在这样一夜，从窗口望出去，你看到的是仿佛望不到头的一片粼粼水面；夜里初到此地的人，会惊叹伦敦这个不起眼的角落竟有这般辽阔空旷。你很容易把自己想象在某座东方城市里。哈默史密斯、奇西克（Chiswick）和巴恩斯（Barnes）披着一袭阴影与银辉织成的浪漫外衣。对岸小树的倒影雕镂在水里，真像一排排倒映的棕榈。远处工厂衬在天际的轮廓，带着清真寺一般的敬畏与神秘。伦敦车马的隐隐低吟里，有一种东方市镇的调子——又慵懒又阴险，藏着杀机，却又安然沉静。而此刻，没有拖船驶过，万籁俱寂，那条无声的河平滑地奔涌着，直灌进伦敦黑色的心脏；在玛杰丽眼里，它有几分尼罗河那种阴沉的庄严，暗示着种种幽暗的无以名之的事物——情欲与死亡，鬼祟的残忍，还有那一份隐秘与罪案的气息——全世界大城市的河岸，都挥不去这气息。

玛杰丽懒懒地想着：那些关于泰晤士河的传闻，究竟有多少是真的？她心爱的河边，如今是否还在暗地里干着可怕的事，被河掩藏，被河纵容，顺流带往大海。……多半是在下游的码头里吧。……沃平那一带。

一艘拖船平平无奇的突突声，破了玛杰丽想象的魔咒。她上下游地来回找斯蒂芬的船，心里因为他回来得这样晚，微微有些恼。她上了床。她困了，可她还要看看书、打个盹，一直等到他进来。

===Original===

She woke first drowsily to the hollow sound of oars clattering in a boat, a murmur of low voices and subdued splashings﻿ ﻿… Stephen mooring the boat﻿ ﻿… how late he was.

A long while afterwards, it seemed, she woke again: Stephen was creaking cautiously up the stairs. She felt that he was peeping at her round the door, murmured sleepily, “How late you are,” dimly comprehended his soft excuses﻿ ﻿… something about the tide﻿ ﻿… caught by the tide﻿ ﻿… engine went wrong﻿ ﻿… of course﻿ ﻿… always did﻿ ﻿… raised her head with a vast effort to be kissed﻿ ﻿… a very delicate and reverent kiss﻿ ﻿… remembered to ask if Cook was back﻿ ﻿… mustn’t lock the front door﻿ ﻿… half heard a deep “Good night, my darling, go to sleep”﻿ ﻿… and drifted luxuriously to sleep again, to comfortable dreams of Stephen, dreams of babies﻿ ﻿… moonlight﻿ ﻿… especial editions﻿ ﻿… palm trees and water﻿—peaceful, silvery water.

Long afterwards there was a distant fretful interruption, hardly heeded. A stir outside. Cook’s voice﻿ ﻿… Stephen’s voice﻿ ﻿… something about Emily. Emily Gaunt﻿ ﻿… not come home﻿ ﻿… must speak seriously to Emily tomorrow﻿ ﻿… can’t be bothered now. Stephen see to it﻿ ﻿… Stephen and Cook. Cook’s voice, raucous. Cook’s night out﻿ ﻿… late﻿ ﻿… go to bed, Cook﻿ ﻿… go to bed﻿ ﻿… go to bed, everybody﻿ ﻿… all’s well.

===Chinese===

她先是迷迷糊糊地醒了一回，听见空咚空咚的桨架声在船上响，低低的絮语，轻轻的水声……斯蒂芬在系船……他可真晚啊。

仿佛过了很久，她又醒了一回：斯蒂芬正小心翼翼、吱呀吱呀地上楼来。她觉出他从门边窥看她，睡意惺忪地咕哝了一句「你可真晚啊」，朦朦胧胧听懂了他轻声的辩解……什么潮水……叫潮水困住了……发动机坏了……可不……老毛病……费了好大的劲才抬起头来讨了个吻……一个极轻柔、极郑重的吻……还记得问了句厨娘回来没有……前门别上锁……迷迷糊糊听见一声低沉的「晚安，亲爱的，睡吧」……便又美美地沉入梦乡，梦见舒心的斯蒂芬，梦见孩子……月光……号外……棕榈树和水——安安静静、银光闪闪的水。

又过了许久，远处起了一场恼人的骚动，她几乎没有理会。外头一阵动静。厨娘的声音……斯蒂芬的声音……说的是埃米莉。埃米莉·冈特……还没回家……明天得跟埃米莉严肃地谈谈……这会儿顾不上。让斯蒂芬去管……斯蒂芬和厨娘去管。厨娘的声音，沙哑。厨娘出去休假的晚上……晚了……睡去吧，厨娘……去睡吧……都去睡吧，各位……万事大吉。

---

===Original===

Stephen turned out the light and crept away to the little room behind, thanking God for the fortunate sleepiness of his wife. The dreaded moment had passed.

He sat down wearily on the bed and tried to reduce the whirling tangle in his brain to order. He ought, of course, to be thinking things out, planning precautions, explanations, studied ignorances. But he was too muddled, too tired. God, how tired! Lugging that hateful sack about. And that awful row home﻿—more than a mile against the tide, though John had done most of that, good old John.﻿ ﻿… (There was something disturbing he had said to John, when they parted at last﻿—what the devil was it?﻿ ﻿… Something had slipped out.﻿ ﻿… An intangible, uneasy memory prodded him somewhere﻿ ﻿… no matter.) And then when he did get back, what a time he had had in the scullery, tidying the refuse on the floor, groping about under a table﻿ ﻿… hundreds of pieces of paper, grease-paper, newspaper, paper bags, orange skins, old tins, bottles.﻿ ﻿… He had gathered them all and put them in a bucket, a greasy bucket, with tea-leaves at the bottom﻿ ﻿… carried it down to the river on tiptoe﻿ ﻿… four journeys. God, what a night!

But it was over now﻿—it was over﻿—that part of it. All that was wanted now was a straight face, a little acting, and some straightforward lying. “God knows, I can lie all right,” Stephen thought, “though nobody knows it.” What lie was it he had invented about the sack, tired as he was? Oh yes, that John had borrowed it, and that John had first emptied the rubbish into the river.﻿ ﻿… Yes, he had coached John on the steps about that﻿ ﻿… told him to keep it up if necessary. Old John had looked funny when he said that. John didn’t like lies, even necessary ones. A bit of a prig, old John.

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬关了灯，溜进后头那间小屋，暗暗感谢上帝，他妻子睡得这样巧。提心吊胆的那一刻总算过去了。

他疲惫地在床沿坐下，想把脑子里那团乱转的乱麻理出个头绪来。按说，他此刻应该把事情细细想清楚，盘算好预防的措施、说辞，还有精心做作的「一无所知」。可他太乱了、太累了。天哪，累成这样！拖着你那个可恨的口袋挪来挪去。还有回家那段要命的水路——顶着潮水一英里还多，好在大部分是约翰划的，好样的老约翰。……（分手的时候，他跟约翰说过一句什么不妙的话——到底是什么来着？……有什么话溜出了口。……一段说不清的、不安的记忆在什么地方捅他……算了，不管它。）等他终于到家，在洗涤间里又是一阵好忙——收拾地上的垃圾，在桌子底下摸来摸去……几百张纸，油纸、报纸、纸袋、橘子皮、旧铁罐、瓶子。……他把它们统统收拢，装进一只桶，一只油腻腻的桶，桶底还压着茶叶渣……踮着脚一趟趟提下河边……跑了四趟。天哪，这算什么夜晚！

可如今都过去了——过去了——那一节总算过去了。眼下要的只是一张一本正经的脸，来一点表演，再加上几句直截了当的谎话。「天知道，我撒谎的功夫是不坏的，」斯蒂芬想，「只是谁都不知道罢了。」那只口袋的事，他累成那样，编了个什么谎来着？哦，对了：说约翰把它借走了，又说里头的垃圾是约翰先倒进河里的。……对，他在台阶上就这个给约翰对的口供……嘱咐他必要的时候就咬死这一套。他说这话的时候，老约翰那副神情可真好笑。约翰不喜欢撒谎，必要的谎也不喜欢。老约翰这人，是有点儿道学的。

===Original===

Stephen pulled at the bow of his black tie and fumbled at the stud. He took off one sock and scratched his ankle reflectively. It was a pity about John. He was such a good fellow, really, such a good friend. He had helped him splendidly tonight, invaluable. But God knew what he felt about it all.﻿ ﻿… Shocked, of course.﻿ ﻿… Flabbergasted (whatever that meant). The question was, how would he get over the shock? How would he feel when he woke up? Would he be permanently shocked, stop being friends?﻿ ﻿… He was a friend worth keeping, old John. And his opinion was worth having, his respect. Anyhow, it was going to be awkward. One would always feel a bit mean and ashamed now with John﻿—in the wrong, somehow.﻿ ﻿… Stephen hated to feel in the wrong.

Cook lumbered breathlessly up the stairs, and halted with a loud sigh on the landing. She knocked delicately on Mrs. Byrne’s door and threw out a tentative, “If you please, mum.” Stephen went out. The acting must begin.

“What is it, Mrs. Beach﻿—speak low﻿—Mrs. Byrne’s asleep.”

“It’s Emily, sir, if you please, sir, turned half-past eleven now, sir, and she’s not in the house. I didn’t speak before, sir, thinking she might have slipped out like for a bit of a turn and met a friend like. She weren’t in the kitchen, sir, when I come in, nor in the bedroom neither. I thought perhaps as how you’d seen her, sir, when you come in and sent her on a herrand like. What had I best do sir shall I lock up sir it’s late for a young girl and gone out without her mack too.”

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬扯开黑领带的结，去摸领口的纽扣。他脱下一只袜子，若有所思地挠着脚踝。约翰的事，可惜了。他真是条好汉，真的，真够朋友。今晚他帮了大忙，宝贵得很。可天知道他心里对这一切作何感想。……自然是吓坏了。……惊得目瞪口呆（管它究竟什么意思）。问题是，这一惊他缓得过来么？明早醒来他会是什么感觉？会不会从此惊魂不定，朋友也做不成了？……这个朋友值得留住，老约翰。而且他的看重、他的敬重，都是值钱的东西。不管怎样，往后要尴尬了。打今儿起，跟约翰相处，总会有点心虚、有点羞惭——不知怎么，总归是理亏了。……斯蒂芬最恨理亏的感觉。

厨娘气喘吁吁、沉甸甸地爬上楼来，在楼梯口一声长叹站住了脚。她在伯恩太太门上轻轻敲了敲，试探着丢出一句「太太，对不起您哪」。斯蒂芬走了出去。该开场演戏了。

「什么事，比奇太太（Mrs. Beach）——小点声——伯恩太太睡着了。」

「是埃米莉的事，先生，恕我回话，先生，都过十一点半了，先生，她还没进家门。我先前没言语，先生，寻思她兴许像平常那样溜出去遛个弯儿，会个朋友去了。可我进屋那会儿，厨房里没有她，先生，卧房里也没有。我寻思兴许您回来的时候见着她了，先生，打发她出去办个事什么的。您说我该怎么办才好先生要不要锁门先生大姑娘家这么晚不回来出门连件雨衣也没带。」

===Original===

Mrs. Beach concluded her remarks with a long, unpunctuated peroration as if fearful that her scanty wind should fail altogether before she had fully delivered herself.

Stephen thought rapidly. Had he sent Emily out on a “herrand,” or had he not seen her at all?

He said, “No, Mrs. Beach, I didn’t see her; I went straight out on to the river. No doubt she went out for a little walk and met a friend, as you say. She’ll be back soon, no doubt, and I’m afraid you’ll have to let her in﻿ ﻿… very naughty of her to stay out so late. Nothing to be done, I fear. Good night, Mrs. Beach.”

===Chinese===

比奇太太用一长串不加标点的收场白结束了这番话，仿佛生怕她那口本来就不富裕的气，还没等把话倒完就彻底断了。

斯蒂芬飞快地盘算。他到底是打发过埃米莉出去「办个事」，还是压根儿就没见过她？

他答道：「没见过，比奇太太，我没看见她；我径直上河去了。不用说，她正像您说的，出去散了会儿步，遇上个朋友。她很快就会回来的，只怕到时候还得劳您去开门……这么晚还待在外头，太不像话了。我想，是没有法子的事。晚安，比奇太太。」

===Original===

Mrs. Beach caught sympathetically at Stephen’s meaning suggestion of Emily’s naughtiness. “Good night, sir,” she puffed; “she always was a one for the young men, though I says it myself, but there youth will ’ave its fling, they say, and sorry I am to disturb you, sir, but I thought as I’d best speak, it was that late, sir.”

“Quite right, Mrs. Beach. Good night.”

“Good night, sir.”

Mrs. Beach sighed herself ponderously down the dark stairs. Stephen went back into his room with a startling sense of elation. He had done that well. It would be marvellously easy if it was all like that. That word “naughty” had been a masterpiece; he was proud of it. Already he had set moving a plausible explanation of Emily’s disappearance﻿—Emily’s frailty﻿—Emily’s “friend.” Cook would do the rest. Mentally he chuckled.

===Chinese===

比奇太太立刻会意，顺着斯蒂芬话里点出的埃米莉的「不像话」接了过去。「晚安，先生，」她喘着气说，「她向来就是见了年轻男人挪不动步的那路人，这话是我自己说的；可人家不常讲嘛，年轻人总要疯一疯；打搅您真对不住，先生，可我寻思还是回一声的好，实在是那么晚了，先生。」

「很对，比奇太太。晚安。」

「晚安，先生。」

比奇太太叹着气，沉甸甸地挪下黑黢黢的楼梯去了。斯蒂芬回到自己房里，心头涌起一阵惊人的得意。这一手他干得漂亮。要是事事都这样，可就轻松得出奇了。「不像话」这个词是个杰作；他真为它自豪。埃米莉失踪的解释，这就算推动起来了——一个说得过去的解释——埃米莉的不自重——埃米莉的「朋友」。剩下的自有厨娘去张罗。他在心里窃笑。

===Original===

Suddenly then he appreciated the vileness on which he was congratulating himself, and the earlier blackness settled upon him. Something like conscience, something like remorse, had room to stir in place of his abated fears. It was going to be a wretched business, this “easy” lying and hypocrisy and deceit﻿—endless stretches of wickedness seemed to open out before him. What a mess it was! How the devil had it happened﻿—to him, Stephen Byrne, the reputed, respectable young author?

Suddenly﻿—like the lights fusing﻿ ﻿… What, in Heaven’s name, had made him do it? Emily Gaunt, of all people.﻿ ﻿… Curse Emily! He wasted no pity on her, no sentimental sorrow for the wiping out of a warm young life. Emily had brought it on herself, the little fool. It was her fault﻿—really.﻿ ﻿… Stephen was too self-centred to be gravely disturbed by thoughts of Emily, except so far as she was likely to affect his future peace of mind. And he had seen too much of death in the war to be much distressed by the fact of death. His inchoate remorse was more of a protest than a genuine regret for wrong﻿—a protest against the wounding of self-respect, against the coming worries and anxieties and necessary evasions, and all the foreseen unpleasantness which this damnable night had forced upon him. It must not happen again, this kind of thing. Too upsetting. Stephen began to make fierce resolutions, as sincere as any resolutions can be that rest on such unsubstantial foundations. He was going to be a better fellow in future﻿—a better husband.﻿ ﻿… People thought a lot of him at present﻿—and they were deceived. In future he would live up grandly to “people’s” conception of him, to Margery’s conception of him.

===Chinese===

跟着他猛然醒悟，自己正在道贺的这份勾当有多下作，先前那片黑暗重新罩了下来。某种像良心的东西、像悔恨的东西，趁着退了潮的恐惧，得了活动的空隙。这将是一桩够受的营生，这桩「轻松」的撒谎、虚伪和欺骗——没完没了的恶行，仿佛在他面前一程程铺展开去。这叫什么烂摊子！见鬼，怎么会闹到这一步——偏偏是他，斯蒂芬·伯恩，那位众口相传、体面堂堂的年轻作家？

突然——就像保险丝烧断了一样……老天爷在上，究竟是什么叫他干出这事的？埃米莉·冈特，这么多人里偏偏是她。……埃米莉这该死的！他不为她浪费一丝怜悯，不为一条温热的年轻生命就这么抹掉，洒一滴感伤的泪。埃米莉是自找的，这个小蠢货。是她的错——真的。……斯蒂芬太以自我为中心，想到埃米莉并不会叫他怎样不安，除非她多半会搅扰他将来的心境。何况大战里死人的场面他见得太多，死亡这件事本身已经够不着他的痛处。他那一星半点的悔恨，与其说是真心悔罪，不如说是一场抗议——抗议自尊心受了伤，抗议眼看要来的烦恼、忧惧和躲不开的遮遮掩掩，抗议这个可诅咒的夜晚强塞给他的一切可以预料的不痛快。这种事，往后绝不能再有。太折腾人了。斯蒂芬开始下狠心的决心，这些决心有多真诚，一切筑在这样虚浮的地基上的决心就有多真诚。往后他要做个更好的人——更好的丈夫。……眼下人们把他看得很高——而他们都受骗了。今后他要堂而皇之地活到「人们」心目中的他那个高度，活到玛杰丽心目中的他那个高度。

===Original===

When he thought of Margery he was suddenly and intensely ashamed. That aspect of his conduct he had so far managed to ignore. Now he became suddenly hot at the thought of it. He had behaved damnably to Margery. Supposing she had come back earlier, discovered Emily. “A‑a‑ah!” A strangled exclamation burst from him, as men groan in spite of themselves at some story of brutality or pain. Sweat stood about his temples. Poor Margery, so patient and loving and trustful. What a swine he had been! The resolutions swelled enormously﻿ ﻿… no more drinking﻿ ﻿… the drink had done it﻿ ﻿… he would knock it off altogether. No, not altogether﻿—that was silly, unnecessary. In moderation. He slipped his trousers to the floor.

Margery thought too much of him, believed in him too well. It was terrible, in a way, being an idol; life would be easier if one had a bad reputation, even an ordinary “man-of-the-world” reputation. A character of moral perfection was a heavy burden, if you were not genuinely equal to it. Never mind, in future, he *would* be equal to it; he *would* be perfect. Tender and chivalrous thoughts of Margery invaded him; the resolutions surged wildly up, an almost religious emotion glowed warmly inside him; he felt somehow as he used to feel at Communion, walking back to his seat. He used to pray in those days, properly.﻿ ﻿… He felt like praying now.

===Chinese===

一想到玛杰丽，他猛然羞愧得无地自容。他行止的这一层，他至今总算想法子绕开了没想。此刻一想起，他浑身上下腾地烧了起来。他对玛杰丽干下的事，真是天理难容。万一她回来得早，发现了埃米莉呢。「啊——啊——啊！」一声扼住的喊从他嗓子里迸出来，就像男人听见什么残暴或惨痛的故事，不由自主地哼出声一样。汗珠子挂满了鬓角。可怜的玛杰丽，那么耐心，那么深情，那么信任人。他做了怎样一头猪啊！决心霎时间胀得极大……戒酒……全是酒招的……干脆一滴不沾。不，不必一滴不沾——那样太傻，也犯不上。有节制就行。他把裤子褪到了地板上。

玛杰丽把他想得太好了，把他信得太足了。做一尊偶像，从某方面说真是可怕；名声要是糟一点，日子倒好过些，哪怕只是寻常那种「见过世面的男人」的名声。一副道德完美的品格是一副重担，倘若你并非真能扛得动它。不要紧，往后他*一定*扛得动；他*一定*做到完美。对玛杰丽的柔情和骑士式的念头一阵阵袭上心头；决心狂野地往上涌，一种近乎宗教的感情在他心里暖烘烘地发着光；他隐隐觉得，自己就像从前领完圣餐、走回座位时那样。那时候他还祷告，规规矩矩地。……这会儿他想祷告了。

===Original===

He tied the string of his pyjamas and knelt down by the small bed. It was a long time since he had prayed. During the war, in tight corners, when he had been terribly afraid, he had prayed﻿—the sick, emergency supplications of all soldiers﻿—the “O God, get me out of this and I will be good” kind of prayer. The padres used to preach sermons about such prayers, and sometimes Stephen had determined to pray always at the safe times as well as the dangerous, but this had never lasted for long. Now his prayers were on the same note, wrung out of him like his resolutions by the urgent emotions of the moment, sincere but bodiless.

He prayed, “O God, I have been a fool and a swine. O God, forgive me for this night’s work and get me out of the mess safely, and I will﻿—I will be good.” That was the only way of expressing it﻿—“being good,” like a child. “In future I will be a better man and pray more often. O God, keep this from Margery, for her sake, not mine. O God, forgive me, and make me better. Amen.”

===Chinese===

他系上睡衣裤的带子，在那张小床旁边跪下。他已经很久没有祷告了。大战那会儿，在走投无路的关口，怕得要死的当儿，他祷告过——那是当兵的人都会的那种病急乱投医的求告——「哦上帝，让我脱了这一关，我一定学好」式的祷告。随军牧师们爱拿这种祷告讲道，斯蒂芬有几次也下过决心，太平无事的年月也要照样祷告，可从来撑不长久。此刻他的祷告还是那个旧调门，跟他的决心一样，是叫眼前的急切情绪从身上绞出来的，真诚，却没有形体。

他祷告：「哦上帝，我做了傻瓜，做了猪。哦上帝，饶了我今夜的勾当，叫我平平安安从这摊烂泥里脱身，我就——我就学好。」话只能这么说——「学好」，跟小孩子似的。「往后我要做个更好的人，也要多祷告。哦上帝，别让玛杰丽知道这件事，看在她的分上，不是看我的。哦上帝，饶了我，叫我变好。阿门。」

===Original===

Stephen rose from his knees, a little relieved, but with an uncomfortable sense of bargaining. It was difficult to pray without driving a bargain, somehow﻿ ﻿… like some of those wretched hymns:

> “And when I see Thee as Thou art
 

 I’ll praise Thee as I ought,”

for instance, a close, inescapable contract. The old tune sang in his head. But if one prayed properly, no doubt one learned to exclude that commercial flavour.﻿—How hot it was!

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬从膝盖上起来，心里松快了些，却带着一种讨价还价的不自在。祷告而不讲价钱，不知怎么总是难的……就像有些要命的赞美诗那样：

> 「及至我见祢真体，
 
 
 必照所当称颂祢，」

比如这两句，就是一份扣得严严实实、想赖也赖不掉的契约。那支老调子在他脑子里唱了起来。不过，祷告若是得法，想必便能修到把那股买卖味儿涤除净尽。——真热啊！

===Original===

He turned out the light and crept slowly under the sheets. For a long time he lay staring at the dark, thinking now of Emily’s nightdress.﻿ ﻿… Probably it was marked﻿—in neat red letters﻿—Emily Gaunt. Probably the sacking would wear away where the rope went through it, dragging with the tide. Probably.﻿ ﻿… Hideous possibilities crowded back and gloom returned to him. And what was it he had said to John? He had forgotten about that. Something silly had slipped out, when John had looked so shocked, something intended to soothe John’s terrible conscience, something about “doing the right thing afterwards”﻿—after the baby had safely come. “I’ll put things right then,” he remembered saying. What the devil had he meant by that? What did John think he had meant? Hell!

===Chinese===

他关了灯，慢慢钻进被单里。他睁着眼望黑暗望了许久，这会儿想起的是埃米莉的睡衣。……八成上面缝着记号——细细的红字母——埃米莉·冈特。八成那粗麻布会让绳子穿过的那几处磨破，叫潮水一路拖着磨。八成。……种种狰狞的可能性涌了回来，晦暗重新罩住了他。还有，他跟约翰到底说了句什么来着？他把那个忘了。当时约翰一脸惊骇，有什么蠢话溜出了口——本是想着安抚安抚约翰那可怕的良心——说的是「事后再做该做的事」之类——等孩子平安生下来之后。「到时候我会把一切都补正过来的。」他记得自己是这么说的。他那话到底他妈的是什么意思？约翰又会当成什么意思？见鬼！

===Original===

Stephen threw off the blanket; he was sweating again.

When the cold chime of St. Peter’s struck three he lay still maddeningly awake in a feverish muddle of thought. Then at last he slept, dreaming wildly.

Emily Gaunt shifted uneasily in her oozy bed, tugging at her anchor, as the tide rolled down.

===Chinese===

斯蒂芬一脚蹬开毯子；他又出汗了。

圣彼得广场的钟冷冷地敲响三点，他还直挺挺躺着，烦躁地醒着，脑子烧成一团乱麻。后来他终于睡着了，乱梦纷纭。

潮水滚滚退落，埃米莉·冈特在她那泥泞的床上不安地挪动着，扯拽着她的锚。

## V / 五

===Original===

Every misfortune which can happen to a man who travels Underground in London had happened to John Egerton. Worn and irritable with a sultry day at the Ministry he had jostled with a shuffling multitude on to the airless platform at Charing Cross. From near the bottom of the stairs he saw that an Ealing train was already in; more important, the train was stopping at Stamford Brook. Stamford Brook was a “nonstop station,” so that if you missed your train in the busy hours you might wait for an intolerable time. On this sweltering evening it was urgent to escape as quickly as possible from the maddening crowd of sticky citizens and simpering girls. It was urgent to catch that train. Already they were slamming home the doors. John made a nightmare attempt to hurry down the last few steps and across to that train. His way was blocked by a mob of deliberate backs, unaccountably indifferent to the departure of the Ealing train, and moving with exasperating slowness. John, with mumbled and insincere apologies, dived through the narrow alley between a portly man and a portly woman. Whistles were blowing now, but once down the stairs the way would be fairly clear to the desirable train. Only round the foot of the stairs hovered a bewildered family, a shoal of small children clinging to their expansive mother and meagre sire, wondering stupidly what they ought to do next in this strange muddle of a place. They were back from some country jaunt and bristled with mackintoshes and small chairs and parcels and spades and other impassable excrescences.

John governed himself and said, “Excuse me, please,” with a difficult assumption of calm. None of them moved. John longed to seize the little idiots by the throat and fling them aside, to knock down the meagre man and trample upon him. Instead, he shouted aggressively, “Let me pass, please!”﻿—the train was moving now. The large woman looked back, with a frightened air, shot out an arm with a sharp “Mabel!” and plucked her firstborn daughter aside by the flesh of her arm pinched painfully between finger and thumb. The child screeched, but the way was clear, and John flung forward. An open door was moving almost opposite him; he had only to swing himself in. Then from nowhere appeared a youthful uniformed official, who barred the way with an infuriating aspect of authority, and slammed fast the receding door. The train slid clattering past and vanished with a parting flicker of blue flashes. The boy walked off with an Olympian and incorruptible air, not looking at John, as who should say, “Tamper not with me.” Interfering ass! John had an impulse to go after and abuse him, demonstrate with fierce argument the folly of the youth. The waiting crowd observed him with the heartless amusement of crowds, hoping secretly that he would lose his temper, provide entertainment. John saw them and controlled himself, thinking with a conscientious effort, “His duty, I suppose,” and contented himself with a long glower at the obstructive family.

The next train was a Wimbledon one; the next an Inner Circle; the next a Richmond, not stopping at Stamford Brook. The endless people shuffled always down the stairs, drifted aimlessly along the platform, jostled and barged good-humouredly about the teeming trains. Government flappers congregated giggling in small groups, furtively examined by ambulant young men. In spite of the heat and the stuffy smell of humanity and the exasperation of crowded travelling there was a pleasant atmosphere of contentment and goodwill. Only here and there were the fretful and distressed, mainly countryfolk, unaccustomed to the hardships of London. Tonight the equable John was among these petulant ones, which was unusual. He was worried and depressed﻿—in no mood for a prolonged entanglement with a hot crowd. Never had he waited so long. Number 1 on the indicator now was a Putney train; Number 2 another Inner Circle﻿—what the devil did they want with so many Circle trains? And why was Stamford Brook a nonstop station? Hundreds of people used it﻿—far more than Sloane Square, for example, or St. James’ Park. He would write a letter to the Company about these things. The terms of his letter began to frame themselves in his mind﻿—conceived in the best Civil Service style: “It is evident﻿ ﻿… convenience of greatest number of passengers﻿ ﻿… revised program﻿ ﻿… facilities﻿ ﻿… volume of traffic﻿ ﻿…” The Putney train racketed away; Number 2 was an Ealing now. John edged up to the glaring bookstall and stood with a row of men staring idly at the dusty covers of old sevenpennies﻿—price two shillings. None of these men bought anything, only stood silent and gazed, as if in wonder at such a multitude of unbuyable books. On the cover of one of them﻿—*Three Years with the Hapsburgs: The Thrilling Chronicle of an English Governess*﻿—the gaudy picture of a young woman caught his eye. It reminded him somehow of Emily Gaunt, and he turned away. He did not want to be reminded of Emily.

===Chinese===

在伦敦乘地铁可能降临到一个人头上的种种厄运，约翰·埃格顿一样不落，全赶上了。部里熬过一个闷热的天，他被磨得疲惫烦躁，又跟一大群拖着脚步的人推推搡搡，挤上了查令十字那座不透气的站台。离楼梯底下不远处，他就看见一列开往伊灵的车已经进了站；更要紧的是，这趟车在斯坦福德布鲁克停。斯坦福德布鲁克是个「过站不停」的站，高峰时段你要是误了车，兴许就得等上叫人无法容忍的工夫。在这个热得发昏的傍晚，必须尽快逃离那群黏糊糊的市民和忸怩傻笑的姑娘——这拨人能把人逼疯。必须赶上那趟车。车门已经在砰砰地关死了。约翰做了一次噩梦般的冲刺，想抢下最后几级台阶，冲向那趟车。他的去路却被一堵不慌不忙的后背堵死了——这些后背对伊灵车的开拔出奇地漠不关心，挪动起来慢得叫人冒火。约翰嘴里咕哝着毫无诚意的道歉，从一位胖先生和一位胖太太之间的窄缝里钻了过去。哨子已经吹响，不过只要下到楼梯底下，通往那趟可心列车的路就相当空了。偏偏在楼梯脚下徘徊着一户晕头转向的人家：一群小孩紧扒着他们心宽体胖的娘和瘦巴巴的爹，傻乎乎地不知道在这个乱成一团的鬼地方下一步该怎么办。他们是刚从乡下野餐回来，浑身支棱着雨衣、小折椅、包裹、小铲子和种种无法穿越的多余物件。

约翰按捺住自己，装出一副好不容易维持住的平静，说：「劳驾，借过。」他们一个也没动。约翰真想掐住那帮小蠢货的脖子把他们甩到一边，把那个瘦男人打翻在地再踩上一脚。可他只是恶声恶气地喊：「请让我过去！」——车这时已经动了。那位胖大娘回过头来，一脸惊惶，猛地伸出一条胳膊，尖声喊了句「梅布尔！」，拇指和食指掐着她大闺女臂上的嫩肉，疼得钻心，把她拎到一边。孩子尖叫起来，可路通了，约翰一个箭步冲上前。一扇开着的门几乎正对着他滑过来；他只消一荡身子钻进去就成。这时不知打哪儿冒出来一个穿制服的年轻站务员，摆出一副气死人的权威架势拦住去路，砰地把那扇正渐渐退走的车门关死了。列车哐当哐当地滑过去，闪着一片道别的蓝色电火，消失不见。那小子迈步走开，一脸神明般不受收买的神气，正眼也不瞧约翰，仿佛在说：「休想通融。」多管闲事的蠢驴！约翰一时冲动，想追上去臭骂他一顿，用激烈的论证让这小子明白自己有多蠢。候车的人群怀着人群特有的无情兴味打量着他，暗暗巴望他发作起来，好戏一场。约翰看见了他们，克制住自己，凭良心费劲地想道：「想必是职责所在。」然后只拿对那户拦路人家的一通狠瞪聊以自慰。

下一趟是温布尔登的车；再下一趟是环线；再下一趟是里士满的，偏偏不在斯坦福德布鲁克停。没完没了的人流没完没了地蹭下楼梯，漫无目的地在站台上飘来飘去，围着挤挤挨挨的列车推推搡搡，和和气气地挤作一团。政府机关的时髦姑娘们一小簇一小簇地凑在一起咯咯傻笑，被东游西逛的年轻小伙子们偷偷打量。尽管天热，尽管人味闷得呛人，尽管拥挤的乘车叫人火大，这里却弥漫着一股心满意足、与人为善的宜人气氛。只是间或也有焦躁愁苦的，多半是乡下人，受不了伦敦这份罪。今晚，素来平和的约翰竟也归入了这些闹脾气的人的行列，这可不寻常。他又烦又闷——全没心思跟一发热烘烘的人群长久纠缠。他从没等过这么久。指示牌上的一号现在是帕特尼的车；二号又是环线——他们要这么多环线车到底干什么？还有，斯坦福德布鲁克凭什么是个过站不停的站？几百号人用这个站——比斯隆广场啦、圣詹姆斯公园啦，多用得多了。他要为这些事给铁路公司写信。信的措辞开始在他脑子里自动成形——用的是最地道的公务员文体：「兹事显然……为最大多数乘客之便利……修正方案……各项设施……客流量……」帕特尼的车哐啷啷地开走了；二号现在是伊灵的了。约翰蹭到灯火晃眼的书报亭旁，跟一排男人站在一起，呆呆地盯着那些七便士旧小说落满灰尘的封面——标价两先令。这些人谁也不买什么，只是默默地站着看，仿佛在惊叹世上竟有这么多无人买的书。其中一本的封面上——《与哈布斯堡皇室共处的三年：一位英国家庭女教师的惊险纪事》——一个俗艳的年轻女人的画像攫住了他的目光。不知怎么，它让他想起埃米莉·冈特，他赶紧扭过头去。他不愿想起埃米莉。

===Original===

The Ealing train came in, and John was swept in with a tight mass of people through the middle doors of a smoking carriage. The atmosphere was a suffocating mixture of hot breath and evil tobacco-smoke. The carriage was packed. Men and women stood jammed together like troops in a communication-trench. Here and there a clerk stood up with a sheepish mumble and a sallow woman sank thankfully into his seat. John stared with increasing resentment at the rows of men who did not get up﻿—tired labourers in corduroy trousers who sat on in unmoved contentment, or gross men with cigars who screened themselves behind evening papers, pretending they did not notice the standing women.

The train stopped, and there was a fierce squeezing and struggling at the doors. A man behind John remembered suddenly that he wanted to get out, and began with much heaving and imprecation to hew a passage, treading violently on John’s ankle. But by now there were more people surging inwards, clinging precariously to the fringe of the mob. The train rushed on, and the man was left within it, cursing feebly. John felt glad, maliciously, ridiculously glad. But when he looked again at the sedentary gross men, the placid labourers, and at the short, pale women swaying in the centre he became righteously furious with the evil manners of the men. He felt that he would like to address them, curse them about it﻿—that fat one with the insolent leer and the cap all cock-eye, especially; he would say loudly at the next station, “Why don’t you give one of these ladies your seat?” Then the man would *have* to get up, would stand shamed before the world, while some grateful female﻿—that nurse there﻿—took his seat. Perhaps all the others would follow.

Or perhaps it would happen quite differently. The man would not hear, or pretend not to hear; and he, John, would have to repeat his remark, losing greatly in dramatic force. And everyone would stare at him, as if he were a madman! Or the man would surrender his seat with a sweet smile and an apology, “Very sorry, I didn’t see”; and then the fools of women would refuse to take the seat. They would all say they were getting out at the next station; they would all simper and deprecate and behave like lunatics. The man would hover with a self-righteous, ingratiating smirk and sit down again. And John Egerton would look a fool. No﻿—it couldn’t be done. What cowards men were!

===Chinese===

伊灵的车进了站，约翰被一大团紧实的人流裹着，从一节吸烟车厢中间的门挤了进去。车厢里的空气是滚烫的呼吸和呛人的烟草味混成的窒息物。车厢塞得满满当当。男男女女紧紧挤在一起，像交通壕里的队伍。这儿那儿，偶尔有个职员讪讪地咕哝一声站起来，一个面色菜黄的女人满怀感激地陷进他的座位。约翰带着越烧越旺的怨气，瞪着那一排排不起身的男人——有的穿灯芯绒裤子的疲惫工人，心满意足地坐着，纹丝不动；有的是叼雪茄的肥汉，拿晚报把自己挡起来，装作没看见站着的女人。

车停了，车门口爆发了一场凶猛的挤压与挣扎。约翰身后的一个男人猛然想起自己要下车，便连推带骂地砍出一条通路，狠狠踩在约翰的脚踝上。可这时往里涌的人更多了，七颠八倒地扒在人堆外沿。列车一冲而去，那男人没下去，留在车里有气无力地骂着。约翰觉得高兴——恶毒地、荒唐地高兴。可等他再看那些稳坐的肥汉、安然的工人，再看车厢当中摇摇晃晃的矮小苍白的女人们，他又对男人们这副恶劣行径义愤填膺。他觉得自己很想开口教训他们，为此骂他们一顿——尤其是那个斜眼无礼、帽子歪到一边的胖子；下一站他要大声说：「你为什么不给这些女士中的一位让个座？」那人就*不得不*站起来，在全车人面前丢脸地站着，而某位感激的女子——那边那位护士——坐上他的座位。说不定其余的人都会跟着学。

又或者，事情会全然两样。那人会听不见，或者装作听不见；而他，约翰，只好把话再说一遍，戏剧效果大打折扣。于是人人都盯着他瞧，当他是疯子！再不然，那人会满面春风地含笑道歉，让出座位：「真抱歉，没瞧见」；可接着那帮蠢女人会死活不肯坐。她们全会说下一站就下车；全都咯咯傻笑、推三阻四，跟疯子一个样。那人便挂着一副自命不凡、讨好卖乖的假笑在旁边干耗着，然后重新坐下。而约翰·埃格顿就成了个傻子。不行——这招行不通。男人们多怂啊！

===Original===

A very hot and spotty man breathed disgustingly in John’s face; unable to move his body, he turned his head away to the left. On that side stood a robust young woman, with hatpins menacingly projecting from a red straw hat. Her head rocked as the train jolted: the cherries on her hat bobbed ridiculously, the naked hatpin-points swung backwards and forwards in front of John’s eye. He turned back to the disgusting breath of the spotty man.

At Earl’s Court the crowd melted a little; there were no seats, but there was room to breathe﻿—room to stand by oneself, free from pressure of strange bodies. At Baron’s Court he crept into a seat. At Hammersmith a noisy mob of shopgirls and hobbledehoys surged in, and he surrendered his seat to a young woman, who was munching something. She sat down with a giggle and took her sister on her lap. Together they eyed him, with whispered jocularities. Only two more stations.

The lights were out now. The train ran out through the daylight on to a high embankment, past an interminable series of dingy houses. There was more air. The filthy smoke eddied out of the narrow windows. The train rocked enormously﻿—a bad piece of line. Looking down the car from his place by the door, John saw through the haze an interminable vista of uniform right hands fiercely clinging to uniform straps, of right arms uniformly crooked, of bowed heads uniformly bent over evening papers, of endless backs uniformly enduring and dull. And as the train gave a lurch, all the elbows swung out together towards the windows, and all the bodies bent outward like willows in the wind, and all the heads were lifted together in a mute and uniform protest. It was all like some fantastic physical drill. Then he fell into the weary stupor of the habitual Underground traveller, listening semiconsciously to the insane chatter of the chuckling girls. Ravenscourt Park shot by unnoticed. The train ran on forever.

===Chinese===

一个浑身滚烫、满脸粉刺的男人朝约翰脸上呼哧呼哧地喷着令人作呕的鼻息；他身子动弹不得，只好把头扭向左边。那边站着一个健壮的年轻女人，红草帽上凶巴巴地支棱出几枚帽针。车身一颠一簸，她的头跟着摇晃：帽子上的樱桃可笑地上下蹦跳，光秃秃的帽针尖在约翰眼前荡过来荡过去。他又转回来，领受粉刺男人那令人作呕的鼻息。

到厄尔斯考特，人群化开了一些；还是没有座位，但有了喘气的余地——能独自站住了，不必再受陌生人身体的挤压。到巴伦斯考特，他蹭着坐进一个座位。到哈默史密斯，一大帮吵吵嚷嚷的女店员和愣头愣脑的半大小子涌进来，他把座位让给一个正嚼着什么的年轻姑娘。她咯咯笑着坐下，把妹妹抱上膝头。姐俩一齐拿眼打量他，交头接耳地说笑。只剩两站了。

车灯这会儿熄了。列车驶出洞口进入天光，上了高高的路堤，掠过一长串没完没了的灰扑扑的房屋。空气多了些。肮脏的烟雾从狭窄的车窗里打着旋涌出去。列车剧烈地摇晃——这段路轨糟透了。约翰从靠门的位置顺着车厢望去，透过雾蒙蒙的烟气，看见一幅没完没了的长卷：清一色的右手死攥着清一色的吊带，清一色的右臂弯成清一色的弧，清一色的低头脑袋埋进晚报，还有无穷无尽的脊背，清一色的坚忍而木然。而车身猛一晃，所有的胳膊肘一齐朝车窗甩出去，所有的身体一齐向外弯，像风中的柳树，所有的头一齐抬起来，发起一场无声的、整齐划一的抗议。这一切活像某种荒诞的团体体操。随后他坠入了老资格地铁客那种疲惫的恍惚，半醒半睡地听着咯咯傻笑的姑娘们的疯话。雷文斯考特公园一晃而过，无人留意。列车永远地开下去。

===Original===

Stooping suddenly, he saw the familiar letters of Stamford Brook dashing past at an astonishing speed. Surely﻿—surely the train was stopping. The porters’ room﻿—the ticket collector﻿—the passenger-shelter﻿—the Safety First pictures﻿—the advertisement of What Ho!﻿—the other name-board of the station﻿—the whole station﻿—shot maddeningly past. The train rushed on to the intolerable remoteness of Turnham Green. Hell! John Egerton uttered an audible groan of vexation. *Two* nonstop trains running! It was unpardonable. He had not even thought to look at the nonstop labels on the train at Charing Cross. It was too bad. Another matter for the letter to the Company! The women looked at his scowling face and giggled again, whispering behind their hands.

From Turnham Green you might walk home; but it took nearly twenty minutes. Or if you were lucky you caught a train quickly back to Stamford Brook. As they came into the station, John saw an up-train gliding off on the other side of the same platform. Of course! just missed it! And no doubt the next one would decline to stop at Stamford Brook! Once you began having bad luck on the Underground you might as well give up all hope of improving it that day. You might as well walk. He *would* walk. But how damnable it all was!

He waited with the thick crowd at the ticket gate, fumbling for his ticket in his waistcoat pocket. That was where he had put it﻿—he always did. Always in the same place﻿—as a methodical man should do. But it was not there. It was not in the other waistcoat pocket﻿—nor in his right-hand trouser pocket. “Now, then,” said an aggressive voice behind, and he stepped aside. Lost his place in the queue, now! He put down his dispatch-case and felt furiously in his pockets with both hands. The passengers dwindled down the stairs; he was left alone, regarded indifferently by the bored official. This was a fitting climax to an abominable journey.

===Chinese===

他猛地一俯身，看见「斯坦福德布鲁克」那几个熟悉的字以惊人的速度飞掠而过。应该——应该说这趟车要停的呀。搬运工房——收票员——乘客候车棚——「安全第一」宣传画——《你好哇！》（What Ho!）的广告——站台另一头的站名牌——整座车站——发了疯似的一一掠过。列车一路冲向远得叫人无法忍受的特纳姆格林。见鬼！约翰·埃格顿懊恼得哼出了声。连着*两*趟过站不停！不可原谅。他在查令十字竟然压根没想到去看车身上不停站的标牌。太不像话了。又给那封写给铁路公司的信添了一桩素材！那两个女人看着他阴沉的脸，又咯咯笑起来，掩着嘴交头接耳。

从特纳姆格林可以步行回家；可差不多得走二十分钟。运气好的话，也能很快赶上一趟折回斯坦福德布鲁克的车。进站的时候，约翰看见一趟上行列车正从同一站台的另一侧溜溜地滑走。可不！就差这么一步！而且下一趟八成又拒绝在斯坦福德布鲁克停！在地铁上一旦倒起霉来，当天就不必指望时来运转了。不如走回去。他就走回去。可这一切多么可恶！

他混在密集的人群里，在检票口等着，往马甲口袋里摸车票。票就搁在那儿——他一向搁在那儿。永远搁在同一个地方——有条有理的人就该这样。可票不在那儿。不在另一只马甲口袋里——也不在右边裤袋里。「喂，快点。」身后一个来者不善的声音说，他只得闪到一边。这下好，队里的位置也丢了！他放下公文包，两只手在口袋里狂摸一气。乘客们顺着楼梯渐渐稀落下去；只剩下他一个，被那个百无聊赖的站务员淡淡地打量着。这场可憎的旅程，至此迎来了恰如其分的高潮。

===Original===

He found it at last, lurking in the flap of a tobacco-pouch, and because he had come too far he was forced to pay another penny. There was a preposterous argument. “Putting a premium on inconvenience!”

He walked home at last, cursing foolishly, and adding new periods to his letter to the Company.
